95	### The head turn: how it is built
96	1. **Panorama, not a wider flat photo.** A flat outpaint cannot represent a 44-degree turn: the far edge of a planar image at 70 degrees is stretched 3.7x. The plate is mapped onto a cylinder at its own field of view (estimated ~65 deg horizontal). The room to the RIGHT (window wall, beyond the curtain) is generated as a second *view*, camera rotated ~45 deg right, by gemini-3-pro-image image_to_image with room-v3 as the reference. A 21:9 outpaint is generated too, as glue for the 31-45 deg overlap. All three are projected onto the cylinder and feather-blended in angular order: plate (untouched pixels, full res) -> outpaint wing -> rotated view. Backup if the still views are incoherent: kling-video-v3_0 image_to_video "camera pans right", frames stitched into the cylinder.
97	2. **Facing view is the same plate, centred.** Frame hFOV ~53 deg (matches the monitor size zoom 1.26 gave), yaw 0 aimed at the monitor centre. No left wing is needed because the frame is narrower than the plate. This is the Q3 "screen centred, straight in front" requirement, achieved without remaking the room.
98	3. **Turned view.** Yaw ~44 deg right, pitch -4, roll +1.5 (a head, not a tripod). The monitor's right edge sits ~2 deg inside the frame's left edge: a sliver of bezel, no text (Q2). Frame right edge reaches ~70 deg, all generated room.
99	4. **Renderer: Python (numpy/scipy), offline.** Rotation-only reprojection of a cylindrical panorama to a pinhole view is a per-pixel lookup; 4K frames in ~1s each on CPU. No WebGL in headless Chrome, no new npm dependencies, deterministic, debuggable as PNGs. The camera model lives in TypeScript (`src/pov/headTurn.ts`) and is EXPORTED per frame as JSON; Python consumes that JSON. One model, two consumers, verified by projecting the monitor corners in both and comparing.
100	5. **Only the moving frames are pre-rendered:** turn-away (~0.9s), turn-back (~0.8s), half-turn for card beats (~20 deg, both directions), plus the facing still and the turned still. Motion blur baked by averaging 4 sub-frame samples. Reused for all seven retells (Q4). The 20s+ holds use the stills with the existing 2D living drift.
101	6. **Parallax (phase 2, if the base works):** Depth Anything V2 on CPU gives a depth map for the panorama; the eye is offset ~6 cm during the turn because a head rotates about the neck, not the eye. That is the "3D effect" the flat still cannot give. Implemented as depth-guided inverse mapping in the same Python renderer.
102	7. **DOM overlays are world-anchored.** Every card, bubble, label and the "Look Away" text is a planar rectangle in room coordinates (cylinder angle, height, distance). Its four corners are projected through the same camera and applied with the existing `quadTransform` matrix3d. So the overlays rotate with the head exactly as the room does, and the monitor's Screen composite uses the identical path.
103	
104	### Effects (his "coolest effects possible", each tied to something real)
105	- Gaze-shift blink at the start of the turn-away only (people blink when they shift gaze). Three frames, soft, masks the onset. Never on the return.
106	- Motion blur (baked, 4 samples), stronger at the fastest part of the turn.
107	- Peripheral softness: a blurred copy of the plate under a radial mask, strongest at the frame edge on the monitor side while turned. This is the Q3 "blurred to the side / corner of the eye" permission, and it is optical, not a mask on text.
108	- Exposure adaptation: brightness lifts as the window enters, then settles over ~0.6s. Turning toward a bright window is what a real eye does.
109	- Lens breathing: hFOV widens ~2 deg during the turn (focus relaxes), tightens on return.
110	- Ease profile: fast start, long settle with a 2% overshoot; return is quicker and settles square.
111	- Living drift on holds stays (existing), now in yaw/pitch terms so the overlays move with the room.

1	import React from "react";
2	import { AbsoluteFill, Img, staticFile, interpolate, Easing } from "remotion";
3	import { SEC, LOGICAL_W, LOGICAL_H } from "../rrc/tokens";
4	import { quadTransform, screenQuadPx, centringShift } from "./quad";
5	import type { Chunk } from "../rrc/Stimulus";
6	import type { Seg, Phase } from "./beats";
7	import { phaseAt, phaseProgress, returnAt } from "./beats";
8	
9	/*
10	 * The POV room.
11	 *
12	 * Plate, screen and every floating card live inside ONE camera wrapper, so the
13	 * whole world moves together. That is what makes an annotation read as an
14	 * object in the room rather than an overlay on the frame.
15	 *
16	 * Two rules this file exists to keep:
17	 *  1. The screen is Remotion, never generated. Kling cannot render the stimulus.
18	 *  2. NOTHING HIDES. See below.
19	 *
20	 * ROUND 6 - the blur is gone.
21	 *
22	 * Every chunk he is not reading used to be blurred to 2.6px, and all of them to
23	 * 3.1px while he spoke. The intent was optical attention. The effect was that
24	 * the film demonstrated a screen doing the student's looking-away for them, and
25	 * the drill's own briefing refuses exactly that:
26	 *
27	 *   "While you are working the stimulus, nothing on the screen hides. The whole
28	 *    thing stays readable, which means the only thing stopping you rereading a
29	 *    line while you tell it back is you, looking away from it."
30	 *   "A screen that hides the text for you is a crutch that cannot come with you."
31	 *
32	 * So attention is now carried two ways, neither of which costs legibility:
33	 *   - the CAMERA glances off the page, which is the student's own discipline;
34	 *   - a soft pool of LIGHT travels the line he is reading, which adds emphasis
35	 *     without taking any away from the rest.
36	 */
37	
38	/** How far the eye slides off the text when he looks away, in frame px. */
39	const GLANCE_X = 96;
40	const GLANCE_Y = 26;
41	
42	/**
43	 * Zoom, and how much of the off-centre monitor we buy back with it.
44	 *
45	 * These two numbers trade against each other and are the whole of the framing.
46	 * `centringShift` clamps the pan to whatever the zoom affords, so raising PULL
47	 * past what BASE_ZOOM can pay for does nothing. Fully centring needs zoom 1.45,
48	 * where the monitor stands 994px tall in a 1080 frame and the room disappears.
49	 * 1.26 keeps the desk, the window and the wall while moving the monitor from
50	 * x=654 to x=827 of 1920, which is most of the visible error without turning a
51	 * POV shot into a screen recording.
52	 */
53	const BASE_ZOOM = 1.26;
54	const CENTRE_PULL = 1;
55	
56	const ease = Easing.bezier(0.4, 0, 0.2, 1);
57	
58	/** The standing framing offset. Everything below is relative to this. */
59	function base(frameW: number, frameH: number) {
60	  return centringShift(frameW, frameH, BASE_ZOOM, CENTRE_PULL);
61	}
62	
63	export function cameraAt(
64	  s: Seg, t: number, frameW: number, frameH: number,
65	): { x: number; y: number; zoom: number } {
66	  const p = phaseAt(s, t);
67	  const k = phaseProgress(s, t);
68	  const b = base(frameW, frameH);
69	
70	  if (p === "read") {
71	    // Micro-drift down the Chunk, the way an eye tracks a line. Deliberately
72	    // small: this should be felt, not seen.
73	    return { x: b.x - 6 * k, y: b.y + 7 * k, zoom: BASE_ZOOM + 0.006 * k };
74	  }
75	  if (p === "release") {
76	    // Focus lets go before the eye does.
77	    return { x: b.x + interpolate(k, [0, 1], [-6, -26], { easing: ease }),
78	             y: b.y + interpolate(k, [0, 1], [7, 12], { easing: ease }),
79	             zoom: BASE_ZOOM + 0.006 };
80	  }
81	  if (p === "speak") {
82	    // Glanced off. Settles quickly, then hangs with a slow living drift.
83	    const settle = interpolate(k, [0, 0.14], [0, 1], { extrapolateRight: "clamp", easing: ease });
84	    const drift = Math.sin(k * Math.PI * 1.6) * 6;
85	    return { x: b.x - 26 - (GLANCE_X - 26) * settle + drift,
86	             y: b.y + 12 + (GLANCE_Y - 12) * settle + drift * 0.35,
87	             zoom: BASE_ZOOM + 0.006 - 0.012 * settle };
88	  }
89	  // confirm: the eye comes back to the page
90	  const back = interpolate(k, [0, 0.42], [0, 1], { extrapolateRight: "clamp", easing: ease });
91	  return { x: b.x + interpolate(back, [0, 1], [-GLANCE_X, 0]),
92	           y: b.y + interpolate(back, [0, 1], [GLANCE_Y, 0]),
93	           zoom: interpolate(back, [0, 1], [BASE_ZOOM - 0.006, BASE_ZOOM]) };
94	}
95	
96	/** Squarely on the screen, nothing panned off it. */
97	export function camNeutral(frameW: number, frameH: number) {
98	  const b = base(frameW, frameH);
99	  return { x: b.x, y: b.y, zoom: BASE_ZOOM };
100	}
101	
102	/**
103	 * The review, R6-Q2. He sits back a touch and takes the whole page in.
104	 *
105	 * Pulling back rather than pushing in is the point: this is not another read,
106	 * it is a check that the whole thing is held. The eye covers everything, so the
107	 * camera has no one place to be.
108	 */
109	export function camReview(frameW: number, frameH: number, k: number) {
110	  const b = base(frameW, frameH);
111	  const out = Math.sin(Math.min(1, k) * Math.PI) * 0.024;
112	  return { x: b.x, y: b.y - 4 * Math.sin(k * Math.PI), zoom: BASE_ZOOM - out };
113	}
114	
115	/** Where the eye is, as a pool of light travelling the words. */
116	export type ReadLight = {
117	  /** Chunk numbers being lit, or "all" during the review sweep. */
118	  ns: number[] | "all";
119	  /** 0..1 through the sweep. */
120	  p: number;
121	  /** Words covered by the pool. Wider for a check than for a read. */
122	  spread?: number;
123	};
124	
125	/**
126	 * The stimulus as the drill renders it: everything readable, always.
127	 *
128	 * The only thing added is light. A word is never dimmed, never blurred and
129	 * never marked; the lit words simply have a warm pool sitting on them, the way
130	 * a lamp falls on a page. That is what keeps this from reading as an app
131	 * feature the student will go looking for and not find (Round 1).
132	 */
133	export const POVStimulus: React.FC<{
134	  chunks: Chunk[];
135	  light: ReadLight | null;
136	}> = ({ chunks, light }) => {
137	  // Words are laid out per chunk, but the pool travels the LIT RUN as one
138	  // stream, so it crosses a chunk boundary the way an eye does.
139	  const litNs: number[] =
140	    light === null ? [] :
141	    light.ns === "all" ? chunks.map((c) => c.n) : light.ns;
142	
143	  const runWords: { cn: number; wi: number }[] = [];
144	  for (const c of chunks) {
145	    if (!litNs.includes(c.n)) continue;
146	    c.text.trim().split(/\s+/).forEach((_, wi) => runWords.push({ cn: c.n, wi }));
147	  }
148	  const centre = light && runWords.length ? light.p * (runWords.length - 1) : -1;
149	  const sigma = light?.spread ?? 1.7;
150	
151	  const heatOf = (cn: number, wi: number): number => {
152	    if (centre < 0) return 0;
153	    const idx = runWords.findIndex((r) => r.cn === cn && r.wi === wi);
154	    if (idx < 0) return 0;
155	    const d = (idx - centre) / sigma;
156	    return Math.exp(-d * d);
157	  };
158	
159	  return (
160	    <div
161	      style={{
162	        maxWidth: "52ch", color: SEC.ink, fontFamily: SEC.serif,
163	        fontSize: SEC.stimulusPx, fontWeight: 400, lineHeight: SEC.stimulusLh,
164	        fontOpticalSizing: "none", fontVariationSettings: '"opsz" 14',
165	      }}
166	    >
167	      {chunks.map((c) => (
168	        <span key={c.n}>
169	          {c.text.trim().split(/\s+/).map((w, wi) => {
170	            const h = heatOf(c.n, wi);
171	            const on = h > 0.02;
172	            return (
173	              <span
174	                key={wi}
175	                style={on ? {
176	                  background: `rgba(255,203,126,${(0.46 * h).toFixed(3)})`,
177	                  boxShadow: `0 0 ${(19 * h).toFixed(1)}px ${(10 * h).toFixed(1)}px rgba(255,200,118,${(0.36 * h).toFixed(3)})`,
178	                  borderRadius: 5,
179	                  color: h > 0.35 ? "#0a0f1c" : SEC.ink,
180	                } : undefined}
181	              >
182	                {w}{" "}
183	              </span>
184	            );
185	          })}
186	        </span>
187	      ))}
188	    </div>
189	  );
190	};
191	
192	/** Composites any screen content onto the monitor in the plate. */
193	export const Monitor: React.FC<{
194	  frameW: number; frameH: number; children: React.ReactNode;
195	}> = ({ frameW, frameH, children }) => {
196	  const [p0, p1, p2, p3] = screenQuadPx(frameW, frameH);
197	  return (
198	    <div
199	      style={{
200	        position: "absolute", top: 0, left: 0,
201	        width: LOGICAL_W, height: LOGICAL_H,
202	        transform: quadTransform(LOGICAL_W, LOGICAL_H, p0, p1, p2, p3),
203	        transformOrigin: "0 0",
204	        // The panel is emissive; a touch of light spills past its edges.
205	        boxShadow: "0 0 120px 24px rgba(150,170,255,0.10)",
206	      }}
207	    >
208	      {children}
209	    </div>
210	  );
211	};
212	
213	export const Room: React.FC<{
214	  seg: Seg; t: number; frameW: number; frameH: number;
215	  /* Beats that are not part of the read/glance loop supply their own camera.
216	     During The Recall he is facing a blank screen, not glancing off it, so
217	     inheriting the glance-return would crop the app's own footer. */
218	  cam?: { x: number; y: number; zoom: number };
219	  children: React.ReactNode;
220	}> = ({ seg, t, frameW, frameH, cam: camOverride, children }) => {
221	  const cam = camOverride ?? cameraAt(seg, t, frameW, frameH);
222	  return (
223	    <AbsoluteFill style={{ overflow: "hidden", background: "#0b0a10" }}>
224	      <div
225	        style={{
226	          position: "absolute", inset: 0,
227	          transform: `translate(${cam.x.toFixed(2)}px, ${cam.y.toFixed(2)}px) scale(${cam.zoom.toFixed(4)})`,
228	          transformOrigin: "50% 50%",
229	        }}
230	      >
231	        <Img
232	          src={staticFile("plates/room-v3.png")}
233	          style={{ position: "absolute", inset: 0, width: "100%", height: "100%", objectFit: "cover" }}
234	        />
235	        {children}
236	      </div>
237	    </AbsoluteFill>
238	  );
239	};

242	export type { Phase };

1	/*
2	 * Mapping the replicated app screen onto the monitor in the generated plate.
3	 *
4	 * The quad below was DETECTED from public/plates/room-v3.png, not eyeballed:
5	 * the screen is the largest light, low-saturation region in the image, and its
6	 * four extreme corners were taken from that mask. Left edge 1936px, right edge
7	 * 1668px, so the monitor is turned 13.8% away from the camera. Any new plate
8	 * must be re-detected, never adjusted by hand.
9	 */
10	
11	export type Pt = readonly [number, number];
12	
13	/**
14	 * Screen corners in room-v3.png, normalised 0..1. TL, TR, BR, BL.
15	 *
16	 * Pushed out from the detected centroid by OVERSCAN. The detector finds the
17	 * light region, which stops a pixel or two inside the bezel, and the first
18	 * render showed a grey sliver of the original plate down the right edge. The
19	 * composite must overshoot into the bezel, never fall short of it.
20	 */
21	const DETECTED: readonly [Pt, Pt, Pt, Pt] = [
22	  [0.0712, 0.1198],
23	  [0.6156, 0.1839],
24	  [0.6052, 0.7266],
25	  [0.0752, 0.7500],
26	] as const;
27	
28	const OVERSCAN = 1.042;
29	
30	export const SCREEN_QUAD: readonly [Pt, Pt, Pt, Pt] = (() => {
31	  const cx = DETECTED.reduce((a, p) => a + p[0], 0) / 4;
32	  const cy = DETECTED.reduce((a, p) => a + p[1], 0) / 4;
33	  return DETECTED.map(
34	    ([x, y]) => [cx + (x - cx) * OVERSCAN, cy + (y - cy) * OVERSCAN] as Pt,
35	  ) as unknown as readonly [Pt, Pt, Pt, Pt];
36	})();
37	
38	/** The plate is 5504x3072 (1.792:1), slightly wider than the 16:9 frame. */
39	export const PLATE_W = 5504;
40	export const PLATE_H = 3072;
41	
42	/**
43	 * CSS matrix3d that maps an axis-aligned w x h element onto an arbitrary quad.
44	 *
45	 * Solves the projective transform taking the unit square to (p0,p1,p2,p3),
46	 * then post-multiplies by scale(1/w, 1/h) so the element's own pixel size is
47	 * absorbed. Requires transformOrigin "0 0" on the element.
48	 */
49	export function quadTransform(
50	  w: number,
51	  h: number,
52	  p0: Pt, p1: Pt, p2: Pt, p3: Pt,
53	): string {
54	  const [x0, y0] = p0, [x1, y1] = p1, [x2, y2] = p2, [x3, y3] = p3;
55	
56	  const dx1 = x1 - x2, dx2 = x3 - x2, sx = x0 - x1 + x2 - x3;
57	  const dy1 = y1 - y2, dy2 = y3 - y2, sy = y0 - y1 + y2 - y3;
58	
59	  const den = dx1 * dy2 - dx2 * dy1;
60	  const g = den === 0 ? 0 : (sx * dy2 - dx2 * sy) / den;
61	  const hh = den === 0 ? 0 : (dx1 * sy - sx * dy1) / den;
62	
63	  const a = x1 - x0 + g * x1;
64	  const b = x3 - x0 + hh * x3;
65	  const c = x0;
66	  const d = y1 - y0 + g * y1;
67	  const e = y3 - y0 + hh * y3;
68	  const f = y0;
69	
70	  // columns divided by the element's own dimensions
71	  const m = [
72	    a / w, d / w, 0, g / w,
73	    b / h, e / h, 0, hh / h,
74	    0, 0, 1, 0,
75	    c, f, 0, 1,
76	  ];
77	  return `matrix3d(${m.map((n) => Number(n.toFixed(6))).join(",")})`;
78	}
79	
80	/** The quad in frame pixels, for a frame of the given size (plate covers it). */
81	export function screenQuadPx(frameW: number, frameH: number): [Pt, Pt, Pt, Pt] {
82	  // The plate is scaled to COVER the frame, then centred.
83	  const scale = Math.max(frameW / PLATE_W, frameH / PLATE_H);
84	  const drawnW = PLATE_W * scale, drawnH = PLATE_H * scale;
85	  const offX = (frameW - drawnW) / 2, offY = (frameH - drawnH) / 2;
86	  return SCREEN_QUAD.map(
87	    ([nx, ny]) => [offX + nx * drawnW, offY + ny * drawnH] as Pt,
88	  ) as [Pt, Pt, Pt, Pt];
89	}
90	
91	/**
92	 * How far the camera must translate to bring the monitor to frame centre.
93	 *
94	 * R6: he flagged the screen as off centre, and the frame grabs confirm it. The
95	 * cause is in the plate, not the camera: the detected quad spans x 0.071..0.616,
96	 * so the monitor's centroid sits at x = 0.34 of a plate that gets drawn edge to
97	 * edge. The camera never compensated, so the monitor has always sat a third of
98	 * the way across with dead wall to the right.
99	 *
100	 * The catch is that panning right pulls the plate's LEFT edge toward frame, and
101	 * there is no image beyond it. How far we can pan is therefore capped by zoom:
102	 *
103	 *     maxShift = (drawnW * zoom - frameW) / 2
104	 *
105	 * Fully centring needs zoom >= 1.45, at which the monitor stands 994px tall in a
106	 * 1080 frame and the room around it is gone. That trade is his to make, so this
107	 * takes a `pull` and CLAMPS rather than pretending the full move is available.
108	 * Whatever is left over after clamping is reported in `shortfall` so the caller
109	 * can be honest about it instead of silently under-delivering.
110	 */
111	export function centringShift(
112	  frameW: number, frameH: number, zoom: number, pull = 1, headroom = 8,
113	): { x: number; y: number; shortfall: number } {
114	  const scale = Math.max(frameW / PLATE_W, frameH / PLATE_H);
115	  const drawnW = PLATE_W * scale, drawnH = PLATE_H * scale;
116	  const offX = (frameW - drawnW) / 2, offY = (frameH - drawnH) / 2;
117	
118	  const qcx = SCREEN_QUAD.reduce((a, p) => a + p[0], 0) / 4;
119	  const qcy = SCREEN_QUAD.reduce((a, p) => a + p[1], 0) / 4;
120	  const cx = offX + qcx * drawnW, cy = offY + qcy * drawnH;
121	
122	  // Scaling happens about the frame centre, so the translate needed scales too.
123	  const wantX = (frameW / 2 - cx) * zoom * pull;
124	  const wantY = (frameH / 2 - cy) * zoom * pull;
125	
126	  const maxX = Math.max(0, (drawnW * zoom - frameW) / 2 - headroom);
127	  const maxY = Math.max(0, (drawnH * zoom - frameH) / 2 - headroom);
128	
129	  const x = Math.max(-maxX, Math.min(maxX, wantX));
130	  const y = Math.max(-maxY, Math.min(maxY, wantY));
131	  return { x, y, shortfall: Math.abs(wantX - x) };
132	}

39	/*
40	 * The instructional cut.
41	 *
42	 * Driven entirely by the beat script that align/build_script.py emitted
43	 * alongside the soundtrack, so picture and audio cannot drift.
44	 *
45	 *   card     the picture holds and a step is introduced. His audio is silent.
46	 *   read     SILENT. A pool of light travels the words at his real reading pace.
47	 *   tell     step 2. Begins at the LOOK-AWAY, which is why the camera glances
48	 *            off here rather than at first sound.
49	 *   colour   step 3. Same glance, continuing: tell and colour share one camera
50	 *            move, or the cut would jolt at the boundary between them.
51	 *   review   step 4's lead-in. Silent, whole page lit through once as a CHECK.
52	 *   press    the app's own Space chip depresses. Text goes.
53	 *   recall   the real drill exactly, with the argument rebuilding in the room.
54	 *   press2   the second press. "Press space again and the next one loads."
55	 */
56	export const POVInstructional: React.FC<{
57	  slug: string;
58	  chunks: Chunk[];
59	  script: Script;
60	  cues: Cue[];
61	  recall: RecallSentence[];
62	  audioSrc: string;
63	  where: string;
64	  qIndex: number;
65	  qTotal: number;
66	}> = ({ chunks, script, cues, recall, audioSrc, where, qIndex, qTotal }) => {
67	  const frame = useCurrentFrame();
68	  const { fps, width, height, durationInFrames } = useVideoConfig();
69	  const t = frame / fps;
70	
71	  const beat = script.beats.find((b) => t >= b.start && t < b.end) ?? script.beats[script.beats.length - 1];
72	  const k = beat.end > beat.start ? (t - beat.start) / (beat.end - beat.start) : 1;
73	
74	  // Which step is on screen. Cards name themselves; the rest map from the beat.
75	  const stepOf: Partial<Record<Beat["kind"], StepKey>> = {
76	    read: "read", tell: "tell", colour: "colour", review: "review",
77	    press: "recall", recall: "recall", press2: "recall",
78	  };
79	  const step: StepKey | null = beat.kind === "card" ? beat.step! : (stepOf[beat.kind] ?? null);
80	
81	  // A step is only labelled once its card has played. Where there are no cards
82	  // at all (Q2, Q3) the label carries from the first frame, and a one-line cue
83	  // rides under it the first time each step happens.
84	  const introduced = new Set(

88	  /* The review is NOT one of the four steps and deliberately never gets a card,
89	     so gating it on "has its card played" hid it forever. Found in the check

94	  // Frames since this beat began, for springing the label in.

96	
97	  // The stimulus is gone from the press onward, exactly as the drill does it.
98	  const recalling = beat.kind === "recall" || beat.kind === "settle" || beat.kind === "press2" ||

100	  const pressAnim = (kk: number) =>
101	    interpolate(kk, [0.5, 0.58, 0.72, 0.8], [0, 1, 1, 0], { extrapolateLeft: "clamp", extrapolateRight: "clamp" });
102	  const pressed =

106	  /*

117	      if (into >= m.o && into < m.o + m.d) {

127	  const cue = cueIdx >= 0 ? cues[cueIdx] : null;
128	  const speaking = beat.kind === "tell" || beat.kind === "colour";
129	  const bubbleText = patchText ?? (cue ? cue.text : null);
130	  const showBubble = speaking && !!bubbleText;
131	  // Springs from the start of the patch, or from the start of the cue.
132	  const bubbleFrom = patchText !== null
133	    ? Math.round((t - beat.start) * fps)
134	    : cue ? Math.round((srcT - cue.start) * fps) : 0;
135	
136	  /*
137	   * The camera runs off a synthetic segment built from the beats of THIS gi, so
138	   * read -> tell -> colour is one continuous glance. Building it per beat would
139	   * re-settle the glance at the tell/colour boundary, which would just move the
140	   * glitch rather than fix it.
141	   */
142	  const gi = beat.gi;
143	  const readB = script.beats.find((b) => b.kind === "read" && b.gi === gi);
144	  const tellB = script.beats.find((b) => b.kind === "tell" && b.gi === gi);
145	  const colB = script.beats.find((b) => b.kind === "colour" && b.gi === gi);
146	  const activeNs = beat.chunkNs ?? [];
147	  const seg: Seg = {
148	    gi: gi ?? 0, chunkNs: activeNs,
149	    readStart: readB?.start ?? beat.start,
150	    readEnd: readB?.end ?? beat.start,
151	    saidStart: tellB?.start ?? beat.start,
152	    workEnd: colB?.end ?? beat.end,
153	    readCoverage: 1,
154	  };
155	
156	  /*
157	   * The reading light. Only ever on while he is READING or CHECKING - never
158	   * while he is talking, because during those he has looked away and there is
159	   * no eye on the page to show.
160	   */
161	  const light: ReadLight | null =
162	    beat.kind === "read" ? { ns: activeNs, p: k, spread: 1.7 } :
163	    beat.kind === "review" ? { ns: "all", p: Math.min(1, k * 1.06), spread: 3.4 } :
164	    null;
165	
166	  const cam =
167	    beat.kind === "review" ? camReview(width, height, k) :
168	    ["press", "recall", "settle", "press2", "card"].includes(beat.kind) ? camNeutral(width, height) :
169	    undefined;
170	
171	  // The Recall, rebuilding in the room.
172	  const recallB = script.beats.find((b) => b.kind === "recall");
173	  const inRecall = beat.kind === "recall" || beat.kind === "press2" || beat.kind === "settle";

175	  const shownCount = recallB
176	    ? recall.filter((s) => s.text.trim().split(/\s+/).length >= 3 && recallT >= s.start).length

194	            through={through}

4	
5	/*
6	 * Things that hang in the room.
7	 *
8	 * Technique lifted from the HyperFrames kit's t2-lt-nameplate3d / t2-lb-bubble /
9	 * t2-lb-checkmark cards: a perspective stage, a card leaning in space with real
10	 * side depth, and a LOCAL bloom behind each card rather than one global glow.
11	 * The local bloom is what makes a card read as lit by the room instead of
12	 * pasted onto the frame.
13	 *
14	 * These deliberately do not use the app's palette. They must never be mistaken
15	 * for part of the drill, and here that is guaranteed by geometry: nothing in the
16	 * real drill floats in your room.
17	 */
18	
19	const GLASS =
20	  "linear-gradient(135deg, rgba(255,255,255,.10) 0%, rgba(255,255,255,.035) 38%, " +
21	  "rgba(255,255,255,.014) 66%, rgba(255,255,255,.075) 100%)";
22	
23	const Bloom: React.FC<{ w: number; h: number; rgb: string; a?: number }> = ({ w, h, rgb, a = 0.3 }) => (
24	  <div
25	    style={{
26	      position: "absolute", left: -w * 0.18, top: -h * 0.22,
27	      width: w * 1.36, height: h * 1.44, borderRadius: "50%",
28	      background: `radial-gradient(ellipse at 42% 78%, rgba(${rgb},${a}) 0%, rgba(${rgb},${a * 0.33}) 48%, transparent 74%)`,
29	      filter: "blur(38px)", pointerEvents: "none", zIndex: 0,
30	    }}
31	  />
32	);
33	
34	/**
35	 * A card leaning in the room's space. Children sit on its face.
36	 *
37	 * ROUND 6: deepened, at his request for "more 3d effets and su h". The thickness
38	 * used to be one translucent slab 13px behind the face, which reads as a drop
39	 * shadow rather than as an edge. It is now a STACK of thin slices stepping back
40	 * in Z, each slightly darker, which is what gives an extruded object a visible
41	 * side you can see the light run down. Plus a bright top lip on the face, so
42	 * the near edge catches the room.
43	 *
44	 * Cost is a handful of divs per card and no filters, so it stays cheap to render.
45	 */
46	const SLICES = 7;
47	
48	export const Plate3D: React.FC<{
49	  x: number; y: number; w: number;
50	  tiltX?: number; tiltY?: number;
51	  rgb?: string; enter: number;           // 0..1
52	  /** Extrusion in px. The face sits at Z 0 and the slices step back to -depth. */
53	  depth?: number;
54	  /* Cards that hang in the ROOM are absolutely placed in room space. The
55	     instruction cards are not objects in the room, they are the film stopping
56	     to talk, so they lay out in normal flow and let a flex parent centre them. */
57	  flow?: boolean;
58	  children: React.ReactNode;
59	}> = ({ x, y, w, tiltX = 7, tiltY = -9, rgb = "150,170,255", enter, depth = 15, flow = false, children }) => {
60	  const lift = interpolate(enter, [0, 1], [26, 0], { easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) });
61	  const scale = interpolate(enter, [0, 1], [0.9, 1], { easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) });
62	  /* Cards arrive turned slightly further away and settle square, so the entry
63	     is a movement in space rather than a fade. */
64	  const swing = interpolate(enter, [0, 1], [7, 0], { easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) });
65	  return (
66	    <div style={flow
67	      ? { position: "relative", perspective: 1200, perspectiveOrigin: "50% 50%", zIndex: 4 }
68	      : { position: "absolute", left: x, top: y, perspective: 820, perspectiveOrigin: "34% 58%", zIndex: 4 }}>
69	      <div
70	        style={{
71	          position: "relative", width: w, transformStyle: "preserve-3d",
72	          transform:
73	            `rotateX(${(tiltX + swing * 0.5).toFixed(2)}deg) ` +
74	            `rotateY(${(tiltY - swing).toFixed(2)}deg) ` +
75	            `translateY(${lift.toFixed(2)}px) scale(${scale.toFixed(3)})`,
76	          opacity: interpolate(enter, [0, 0.35], [0, 1], { extrapolateRight: "clamp" }),
77	        }}
78	      >
79	        <Bloom w={w} h={130} rgb={rgb} />
80	        {/* Extruded side depth: thin slices stepping back in Z, darkening as
81	            they go, so the edge reads as a solid side rather than a shadow. */}
82	        {Array.from({ length: SLICES }, (_, i) => {
83	          const f = (i + 1) / SLICES;
84	          return (
85	            <div
86	              key={i}
87	              style={{
88	                position: "absolute", inset: 0, borderRadius: 16, zIndex: 1,
89	                transform: `translateZ(${(-depth * f).toFixed(2)}px)`,
90	                background: `rgba(${rgb},${(0.34 * (1 - f * 0.72)).toFixed(3)})`,
91	              }}
92	            />
93	          );
94	        })}
95	        <div
96	          style={{
97	            position: "relative", zIndex: 2, borderRadius: 16,
98	            background: GLASS,
99	            backdropFilter: "blur(14px) saturate(1.12)",
100	            WebkitBackdropFilter: "blur(14px) saturate(1.12)",
101	            border: "1px solid rgba(255,255,255,0.17)",
102	            boxShadow:
103	              "inset 0 1px 0 rgba(255,255,255,0.30), inset 0 -1px 0 rgba(0,0,0,0.20), " +
104	              "0 26px 60px rgba(0,0,0,0.46)",
105	            padding: "20px 24px",
106	            fontFamily: SEC.sans, color: "#fff",
107	          }}
108	        >
109	          {children}
110	        </div>
111	      </div>
112	    </div>
113	  );
114	};
115	
116	/** What he is saying, arriving as he says it. */
117	export const SpeechBubble: React.FC<{ text: string; enter: number; x: number; y: number }> = ({
118	  text, enter, x, y,
119	}) => (
120	  <Plate3D x={x} y={y} w={560} tiltX={6} tiltY={-11} rgb="150,170,255" enter={enter}>
121	    <div style={{ fontSize: 33, lineHeight: 1.36, fontWeight: 400, letterSpacing: -0.2 }}>{text}</div>
122	    {/* pointer tail, back toward him */}
123	    <div
124	      style={{
125	        position: "absolute", left: 44, bottom: -13, width: 30, height: 30,
126	        background: "rgba(255,255,255,0.075)",
127	        borderRight: "1px solid rgba(255,255,255,0.17)",
128	        borderBottom: "1px solid rgba(255,255,255,0.17)",
129	        transform: "rotate(45deg)", borderRadius: 4,
130	      }}
131	    />
132	  </Plate3D>
133	);
134	
135	/** A tick that draws itself on, next to the thing it confirms. */
136	export const CheckLine: React.FC<{ text: string; enter: number }> = ({ text, enter }) => {
137	  const draw = interpolate(enter, [0, 0.55], [1, 0], { extrapolateRight: "clamp", easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) });
138	  return (
139	    <div style={{ display: "flex", alignItems: "flex-start", gap: 13, opacity: interpolate(enter, [0, 0.2], [0, 1], { extrapolateRight: "clamp" }) }}>
140	      <svg width={26} height={26} viewBox="0 0 36 36" fill="none" style={{ flex: "0 0 auto", marginTop: 3 }}>
141	        <defs>
142	          <filter id="ckGlow" x="-40%" y="-40%" width="180%" height="180%">
143	            <feGaussianBlur stdDeviation="2.4" result="b" />
144	            <feMerge><feMergeNode in="b" /><feMergeNode in="SourceGraphic" /></feMerge>
145	          </filter>
146	        </defs>
147	        <path
148	          d="M6 19 L14.5 27 L30 9"
149	          stroke="#7ee0b0" strokeWidth={4.2} strokeLinecap="round" strokeLinejoin="round"
150	          filter="url(#ckGlow)"
151	          pathLength={1} strokeDasharray={1} strokeDashoffset={draw}
152	        />
153	      </svg>
154	      <span style={{ fontSize: 26, lineHeight: 1.34, color: "rgba(255,255,255,0.94)" }}>{text}</span>
155	    </div>
156	  );
157	};
158	
159	/**
160	 * Springs an entry in, so arrivals feel physical rather than timed.
161	 * Deliberately a plain function, not a hook: these are called inside maps and
162	 * conditionals, where a hook would be illegal.
163	 */
164	export function enterAt(framesSince: number, fps: number, stiffness = 108): number {
165	  if (framesSince < 0) return 0;
166	  return spring({ frame: framesSince, fps, config: { damping: 17, stiffness, mass: 0.7 } });
167	}

6	/*

13	 * Two defects this fixes:

16	 *     his head" - which reads as a documentary about someone else rather than
17	 *     as instruction to the person watching. The app's own lines are already

19	 *
20	 *  2. Two of the four labels were inventions. The film said "Reading" and

28	export type StepKey = "read" | "tell" | "colour" | "recall" | "review";

1	import React from "react";
2	import { interpolate, Easing } from "remotion";
3	import { SEC } from "../rrc/tokens";
4	import { enterAt } from "./Float";
5	
6	/*
7	 * The Recall, made visible.
8	 *
9	 * R6-Q3b / R6-Q4. The screen during The Recall is blank, and that is correct:
10	 * `SectionStage.tsx` shows a bare stimulus area with the footer "Say the whole
11	 * argument back from memory." The film was faithful to that and therefore ran
12	 * 23 seconds of blank white panel with a voice over it. He flagged it twice as
13	 * a missing step, because the part that gets scored looked like dead air.
14	 *
15	 * So the screen stays honestly blank and the argument rebuilds BESIDE it, one
16	 * sentence at a time, timed to `recall/generated/*.recall.timing.json`. Each
17	 * line lands as he says it and STAYS, so by the end the whole argument is
18	 * sitting in the room, visibly reassembled from memory next to a screen that
19	 * is showing nothing. That is the difference between hearing "every chunk
20	 * combined" and seeing it.
21	 *
22	 * Why this does not break the Round 1 rule that annotations must never read as
23	 * app UI: the rule is satisfied by GEOMETRY, not styling. These hang in
24	 * perspective with real thickness and a bloom on the surface behind them, in
25	 * front of the monitor rather than on it. Nothing in the real drill floats in
26	 * your room.
27	 */
28	
29	export type RecallSentence = { index: number; text: string; start: number; end: number };
30	
31	/** Lines shorter than this are beats, not content, and never get their own card. */
32	const MIN_WORDS = 3;
33	
34	export const RecallStack: React.FC<{
35	  sentences: RecallSentence[];
36	  /** Seconds since the recall beat began. */
37	  t: number;
38	  fps: number;
39	}> = ({ sentences, t, fps }) => {
40	  const lines = sentences.filter((s) => s.text.trim().split(/\s+/).length >= MIN_WORDS);
41	  const shown = lines.filter((s) => t >= s.start);
42	  if (shown.length === 0) return null;
43	
44	  /*
45	   * Everything said so far stays up, so the column has to fit the WHOLE argument
46	   * by the end. Size is therefore chosen from the full line count, never the
47	   * count so far, or the stack would visibly shrink under itself as it grew.
48	   *
49	   * Chosen by trying sizes rather than by a fudge factor: the number of lines a
50	   * sentence wraps to depends on the size, and the height depends on the lines,
51	   * so a single formula gets it wrong in both directions. Q1's third sentence is
52	   * 291 characters and was the case that broke the guess.
53	   */
54	  const AVAIL = 500;   // room-space px between the stack top and the page bottom
55	  const WIDTH = 1040;  // overhangs the monitor by a deliberate margin, not an accidental one
56	  let size = 30, gap = 11;
57	  for (; size > 17; size--) {
58	    const cpl = Math.max(20, Math.floor(WIDTH / (size * 0.52)));
59	    const h = lines.reduce(
60	      (a, s) => a + Math.ceil(s.text.length / cpl) * size * 1.34 + 18 + gap, 0);
61	    if (h <= AVAIL) break;
62	  }
63	  const fit = size / 30;
64	
65	  return (
66	    <div
67	      style={{
68	        position: "absolute", left: 250, top: 290, width: WIDTH,
69	        perspective: 1150, perspectiveOrigin: "42% 46%", zIndex: 6,
70	      }}
71	    >
72	      <div
73	        style={{
74	          transformStyle: "preserve-3d",
75	          transform: "rotateX(4deg) rotateY(-7deg)",
76	          display: "flex", flexDirection: "column", gap: Math.round(gap * fit),
77	        }}
78	      >
79	        {shown.map((s) => {
80	          const since = Math.round((t - s.start) * fps);
81	          const e = enterAt(since, fps, 96);
82	          /* Older lines settle back in Z so the newest reads as the live one,
83	             while everything already said stays fully legible. */
84	          const age = shown.length - 1 - shown.indexOf(s);
85	          const back = Math.min(age, 5) * 5;
86	          const dim = interpolate(Math.min(age, 5), [0, 5], [1, 0.72]);
87	          return (
88	            <div
89	              key={s.index}
90	              style={{
91	                transform:
92	                  `translateZ(${(-back).toFixed(1)}px) ` +
93	                  `translateX(${interpolate(e, [0, 1], [34, 0]).toFixed(1)}px)`,
94	                opacity: interpolate(e, [0, 0.4], [0, dim], { extrapolateRight: "clamp" }),
95	                transformOrigin: "0% 50%",
96	              }}
97	            >
98	              <div
99	                style={{
100	                  display: "inline-block",
101	                  background: "rgba(9,11,26,0.80)",
102	                  borderLeft: "3px solid rgba(169,188,255,0.85)",
103	                  borderRadius: "4px 10px 10px 4px",
104	                  padding: `${Math.round(9 * fit)}px ${Math.round(17 * fit)}px`,
105	                  fontFamily: SEC.sans,
106	                  fontSize: size,
107	                  lineHeight: 1.34,
108	                  letterSpacing: -0.2,
109	                  color: "#fff",
110	                  boxShadow: "0 18px 40px rgba(0,0,0,0.42)",
111	                }}
112	              >
113	                {s.text}
114	              </div>
115	            </div>
116	          );
117	        })}
118	      </div>
119	    </div>
120	  );
121	};
122	
123	/**
124	 * The count, so the accumulation has a number on it.
125	 *
126	 * Sits under the stack and only appears once more than one line is up, because
127	 * "1 of 4" next to a single line states the obvious.
128	 */
129	export const RecallProgress: React.FC<{ done: number; total: number; enter: number }> = ({
130	  done, total, enter,
131	}) => {
132	  if (done < 2) return null;
133	  return (
134	    <div
135	      style={{
136	        position: "absolute", left: 250, top: 838, zIndex: 6,
137	        opacity: interpolate(enter, [0, 1], [0, 0.92], { extrapolateRight: "clamp" }),
138	        fontFamily: SEC.sans, fontSize: 21, letterSpacing: 1.6,
139	        color: "#a9bcff",
140	        background: "rgba(9,11,26,0.72)", borderRadius: 7, padding: "7px 13px",
141	      }}
142	    >
143	      {done} OF {total} BACK
144	    </div>
145	  );
146	};
147	
148	/** Eases the whole stack away across the settle beat, once the drill has moved on. */
149	export function stackLeave(k: number): number {
150	  return interpolate(k, [0, 0.75], [1, 0], {
151	    extrapolateLeft: "clamp", extrapolateRight: "clamp", easing: Easing.in(Easing.cubic),
152	  });
153	}

3	 *
4	 * Every beat below comes from public/content/timeline.json, which was produced
5	 * by global Needleman-Wunsch alignment of walkthrough_md against the Whisper
6	 * word stream. Nothing here is authored timing; it is his real run.
7	 *
8	 *   readStart -> readEnd    he is reading the Chunk IN HIS HEAD
9	 *   readEnd   -> saidStart  the release: he stops reading, before he speaks
10	 *   saidStart -> returnAt   he has glanced off and is speaking out loud
11	 *   returnAt  -> workEnd    his eye comes back and what he carried is confirmed
12	 */
13	
14	export type Seg = {
15	  gi: number;
16	  chunkNs: number[];
17	  readStart: number;
18	  readEnd: number;
19	  saidStart: number;
20	  workEnd: number;
21	  readCoverage: number;
22	};
23	
24	export type Phase = "read" | "release" | "speak" | "confirm";
25	
26	/** How long the eye-returns-and-confirms beat runs, in seconds. */
27	export const CONFIRM_S = 3.2;
28	
29	export function returnAt(s: Seg): number {
30	  // Never eat more than a third of the speaking stretch.
31	  const speakLen = s.workEnd - s.saidStart;
32	  return s.workEnd - Math.min(CONFIRM_S, speakLen / 3);
33	}
34	
35	export function phaseAt(s: Seg, t: number): Phase {
36	  if (t < s.readEnd) return "read";
37	  if (t < s.saidStart) return "release";
38	  if (t < returnAt(s)) return "speak";
39	  return "confirm";
40	}
41	
42	export function segAt(segments: Seg[], t: number): Seg {
43	  for (const s of segments) if (t < s.workEnd) return s;
44	  return segments[segments.length - 1];
45	}
46	
47	/** 0..1 through the current phase, for driving camera and focus. */
48	export function phaseProgress(s: Seg, t: number): number {
49	  const p = phaseAt(s, t);
50	  const span =
51	    p === "read" ? [s.readStart, s.readEnd] :
52	    p === "release" ? [s.readEnd, s.saidStart] :
53	    p === "speak" ? [s.saidStart, returnAt(s)] :
54	    [returnAt(s), s.workEnd];
55	  const [a, b] = span;
56	  if (b <= a) return 1;
57	  return Math.min(1, Math.max(0, (t - a) / (b - a)));
58	}
59

I found relevant research on head movement kinematics and gaze shifts. Here are the key findings:

## Head Movement Kinematics and Main Sequence

Head movements, mainly rapid rotations of the skull in the horizontal plane, were measured and dynamic parameters of the trajectories displayed in "main sequence" diagrams. This work relates directly to the Zangemeister and Stark research you inquired about.

## 40-Degree Gaze Shifts

Fixed-amplitude 40° gaze saccades were studied with different initial eye-in-head orientations. The research shows that when a head movement accompanies the gaze shift, the gaze-peak velocity will be reduced, even though the gaze amplitude may remain the same.

## Duration and Timing

The saccadic eye movement begins 25−40 ms before the head movement begins, and it ends just after the onset of the head movement. Additionally, measurement of eye and neck muscle activity indicates that an increase in agonist neck muscle EMG precedes the increase in eye muscle EMG by 20 ms.

## Eye-Head Coordination Patterns

Gaze-shift movements fall into four distinct types with respect to eye-head latency, each one involving particular gaze-shift errors and head acceleration trajectories. The research by Zangemeister and Stark established foundational work in understanding these coordinated eye-head movement patterns during visual orienting.

Based on the web search results, I found information about the atlanto-axial joint and head rotation:

## Atlanto-Axial Joint and Head Rotation

The atlantoaxial joint resides in the upper part of the neck between the first and second cervical vertebrae, known as the atlas and axis, respectively. It is a uniaxial pivot joint that allows for rotation only and is formed by a central pivoting, bony structure that rotates around craniovertebral ligaments.

The axis is called the axis because the odontoid process/dens forms a vertical axis around which the atlas rotates. It is a pivot joint that provides 40 to 70% of axial rotation of the head.

However, the search results do not contain specific information about:
- The exact horizontal distance of the rotation axis location behind the eyes
- Eye translation distances during head rotation
- Quantitative measurements of how far behind the eyes the atlanto-axial joint is positioned

If you need more specific biomechanical data about eye translation and the precise distance measurements during head rotation, you may need to search for peer-reviewed biomechanics or vision science literature that discusses gaze stability and the instantaneous center of rotation during head movements.

I found relevant search results related to eye-head coordination, gaze shifts, head lag, eye latency, and the vestibulo-ocular reflex. Here are the key findings:

## Eye-Head Coordination and Large Gaze Shifts

Natural gaze-shifts incorporate complex coordination of eye-head movements including a saccade toward the target, a more sluggish head movement and usually the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) which keeps the eye on target during the latter parts of the head motion. Gaze shifts vary in the extent of eye and head contribution; a large amplitude and/or an eccentric ocular orbital starting position alter the participation of head movement in the shift.

## Head Lag and Eye Latency

Head movement latencies are greater than eye movement latencies because of dynamic biomechanical lags. The increased dynamic lag of head movement leads to the classical gaze pattern. First a rapid saccadic eye movement directs gaze onto target; a slower head movement follows.

## Vestibulo-Ocular Reflex (VOR)

The vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) is classically associated with stabilizing the visual world on the retina by producing an eye movement of equal and opposite amplitude to the motion of the head. The vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) is greatly reduced at the onset of large eye–head (gaze) saccades and resumes before the end of the saccades to stabilize eye-in-orbit and ensure accurate target acquisition.

The search results reference Freedman and Sparks' work on gaze control coordination (noted in index 6-5), though the full details of their specific contributions appear in citations rather than in the main text of these results.

Based on the search results, here are the key findings regarding gaze-evoked blinks, probability with large gaze shifts, head movement, blink timing, and saccade onset:

## Blink Probability with Large Gaze Shifts

Blink probability increases with gaze amplitude and at a faster rate for head-unrestrained movements. More specifically, orbicularis oculi EMG occurred with 97% of saccadic gaze shifts larger than 33°.

## Temporal Relationship: Blink Timing Relative to Saccade/Gaze Onset

The blinks typically began simultaneously with the initiation of head and/or eye movement. However, when examining head movements with eyes closed, orbicularis oculi EMG activity began an average of 39.3 ms before the start of the head movement.

## Effects on Gaze Kinematics

Gaze-evoked blinks induce robust perturbations in eye velocity, with peak and average velocities reduced, duration increased, but accuracy preserved. The temporal features of the perturbation depend on factors such as the time of blink relative to gaze onset, inherent velocity kinematics of control movements, and perhaps initial eye-in-head position.

## Strategic Coupling with Head Movements

Blinks are strategically shifted to co-occur with head movements, showing an active use of a strategy that 'hides' blinks at moments of unreliable visual input during head movements.

Based on the search results, I found substantial information about the relationship between Donders' law, the Fick gimbal model, head torsion, and gaze shifts, with specific references to the work of Crawford, Ceylan, and Henriques.

## Key Findings:

**Fick Gimbal and Head Orientation:**
During normal gaze shifts where the head acts as a platform for the eye, head orientations behave qualitatively like those of a Fick gimbal, which has a horizontal axis nested within a space-fixed vertical axis.

**Torsional Components:**
The rotation vectors representing 3-D head orientations define a twisted saddle-shaped surface with nonzero torsional components at the oblique facing directions.

**Task-Dependent Shifts in Kinematic Strategy:**
When the head was forced to act like a gaze-pointer (imposed by pin-hole goggles or a head-mounted laser), its twisted surface flattened out to become more Listing-like (Ceylan et al. 2000; Crawford et al. 1999).

**Donders' Law During Gaze Coupling:**
When head movements were dissociated from gaze shifts, Donders' law for the head broke down in favor of a minimum-rotation strategy.

**Applied Context:**
Donders' law has been applied to the head, which is mechanically able to rotate torsionally, but which normally adopts just one torsional angle for any one facing direction.

The research demonstrates that the head's compliance with Donders' law, particularly the Fick gimbal strategy with its characteristic torsional characteristics, is fundamentally dependent on whether the head is serving as a gaze platform or as an independent pointer.

Here are the search results for your query about first-person camera head turn animation in game design:

The results cover several key aspects of first-person camera animation:

**Physics and Animation Principles:**
Effective action animation relies heavily on understanding physics principles such as weight transfer, overshoot, and settling. Many actions don't move to a mechanical stop, they go slightly beyond their final position and then ease or settle back into it. After a fast stop, let the mass pass the target a hair, then relax into the final pose with successively smaller oscillations, like a decaying spring.

**First-Person Camera Specifics:**
Successful first-person animation requires different techniques than those used in third-person work; in particular, strategic use of camera shake to sell impact. The camera should stay outside the character mesh and settle after head or fall motion.

**POV Animation Guidelines:**
Keep the camera near the character's eye line so that the perspective feels like a believable head height and orientation; if the camera goes too high or too low, it may no longer feel like the character's own view. Believability comes from small, motivated camera changes rather than big, flashy moves.

**Head Bobbing and Roll:**
Head bobbing can be done by using tilt and alternating it back and forth between the two sides, though this means a player who doesn't want tilt won't get any head bobbing either.

Based on my web search results, I found relevant information about head movement velocity profiles and gaze shifts. Here's what the research shows:

## Head Movement Velocity Profiles and Gaze Shifts

### Asymmetric Velocity and Acceleration Profiles

For movements with intermediate speed, the relative time to peak velocity shows symmetric velocity and acceleration profiles (approximately 0.5), while slow and ballistic movements create asymmetric profiles with shifted values. Consistent relationships between movement time, peak velocity, maximum acceleration, and maximum deceleration have been observed.

### Head-Eye Coordination in Gaze Shifts

Research on gaze shifts—which combine both eye and head movements—has examined movements of 40-degree amplitude specifically. Studies have simulated rightward gaze shifts of 40° amplitude with different initial eye positions. These studies examined eye, head, and gaze velocities, noting the strong eye-position dependence of the contributions of eye and head to the gaze shift, as well as to the gaze kinematics and head-onset delay.

### Peak Velocity Relationships

A 30-degree amplitude is typically associated with a peak angular velocity of 500 degrees per second for eye movements, which provides a reference point for understanding peak velocity magnitudes in similar movement amplitudes.

### Phase Duration and Acceleration-Deceleration

Velocity profiles of goal-directed movements typically exhibit an invariant acceleration phase followed by a deceleration phase that varies from movement to movement.

The research indicates that head movements during gaze shifts show complex relationships between amplitude, velocity, and the relative timing of acceleration and deceleration phases, with significant variation based on initial eye position and movement characteristics.

Tool result:
# Head Movement Kinematics During Gaze Shifts

## Onset Timing (Latency)

**Monkeys (Triggered movements):** "saccadic eye movement begins 25−40 ms before the head movement begins" (Bizzi et al., 1972a)

**Monkeys (EMG activity):** "increase in agonist...neck muscle EMG _precedes_ the increase in eye muscle EMG by 20 ms" (Bizzi et al., 1972a)

**Humans and Cats:** Head movements can precede saccade onset by ~40ms in some tasks (Guitton et al., 1984)

## Duration and Post-Gaze Movement

**Monkeys (35° gaze shift example):** "head does not stop moving for an additional 250ms and 15° of rotation" after gaze completion (Figure 4 description)

## Amplitude Relationships

**Monkeys (5-80° gaze shifts):** "saccade amplitude did not exceed a functional limit (∼35°)" despite mechanical limits of ±45° (Figure 5)

**Monkeys (45° gaze shifts):** "saccade amplitudes were 40° when the eyes begin deviated away...35° when centered...∼28° when deviated in direction" (Figure 6)

## Velocity Effects

**Monkeys (35° constant amplitude):** "peak gaze velocity was >800°/s...however, gaze velocity declined to ∼400°/s when the head contribution increased" (Figure 8A)

**Monkeys/Humans:** "peak saccade velocity could range from 800°/s to as little as 300°/s" during 30° gaze shifts (Phillips et al., 1995)

## VOR Gain Modulation

**Humans:** "VOR gain was modulated – reduced sometimes by as much as 50% - but never fully suppressed" (Tabak et al., 1996)

**Monkeys:** Variable results ranging from "nearly 80% reduction in VOR gain" to "only a 10% reduction" depending on subject (Cullen et al., 2004)

# Gaze-Evoked Blinks: Key Numerical Data

## Blink Probability by Gaze Amplitude
The study found that "blink probability increased with gaze amplitude and at a faster rate for head-unrestrained movements." Specific probability curves were plotted but exact percentages at each amplitude were not numerically stated in the text.

## Blink Onset Timing
**Head-restrained condition:** Blink onset increasingly lagged gaze onset with larger movements (mean±SD and specific millisecond values not provided in numerical form).

**Head-unrestrained condition:** "Blink and gaze onset times were more likely to be aligned around movement onset even for large amplitude movements," with blink more likely to precede gaze onset.

## Effects on Movement Kinematics

**Reaction time reduction:** "Saccade reaction time was reduced by 11.7±22.4 ms (median: 9.1 ms)" during head-restrained movements; "10.7±19.8 ms (median: 9.3 ms)" for head-unrestrained movements.

**Accuracy (amplitude overshoot):** 
- Head-restrained: "1.10±0.98°; median: 0.98°"
- Head-unrestrained gaze: "1.23±1.30°; median: 0.79°"

**Head velocity increase:** Peak head velocity increased "7.2±6.8°/s (median: 6.9°/s)" and "time to reach peak velocity also increased modestly (mean±sd: 5.5±23.6 ms; median: 5.3 ms)."

**Duration:** Movements with blinks showed prolonged duration, though specific millisecond values weren't quantified numerically.

No Evinger 1994 human-specific numerical data were cited.

Tool result:
# Head Torsion/Roll During Horizontal Rotations

## Fick Gimbal Torsion Predictions
The study found that head rotations follow a **truncated Fick gimbal** model, where "roll was almost strictly a function of the pitch/yaw product." For pure horizontal facing (zero pitch), this model predicts minimal roll, as roll depends on the interaction between pitch and yaw components.

## Measured Torsion Range and Variability
- **Maximum roll rotations**: up to 20° observed across all trials
- **Torsional thickness (σ)**: median of approximately 2° in both conditions
- **Individual variation**: Gimbal scores ranged from approximately -0.5 to -1.8 for the twist parameter (_a_5)

## What "Truncated" Means
The truncated Fick gimbal consists of "a pitch axis nested within a yaw axis" with no independent roll control. This reduces degrees of freedom from three to two—roll becomes dependent on yaw and pitch combinations rather than independently controlled.

## Head Movement Ranges
- **Yaw (horizontal)**: Maximum ~60° rotation
- **Pitch (vertical)**: Maximum ~60° rotation (asymmetrical: extension -60°, flexion smaller)
- **Roll**: Maximum ~20°

## Pitch-Yaw Relationship
Roll increased as a linear function of the pitch-yaw product, with individual differences in slope but consistent mathematical relationship across subjects.

## Temporal Dynamics
Trajectories "consistently followed a sigmoid curve, with the maximum velocity halfway through the rotation," though specific velocity/duration values weren't quantified.

# First-Person Camera Techniques for Head-Like Feel

Based on the article, the author does **not** provide concrete techniques for making a camera feel like a head in the traditional sense. Instead, the approach emphasizes:

## Key Principles Rather Than Head Simulation

The article explicitly discourages lens simulation effects. It states: **"your first-person game camera is not a real physical camera, and almost all the ways you make it seem like one will make your game harder to play."**

## Actual Techniques Mentioned

**Motion-Based Effects:**
- **Head bobbing** (implemented via tilt alternating between directions at 0.5-second intervals)
- **Camera displacement** (blending outward then back to origin, typically 1-2 seconds)
- **Camera shake** (duration-based with random noise vectors)
- **Tilt effects** (limited to avoid motion sickness; optional accessibility toggle)

**Transform Hierarchy:**
The article describes a layered system:
1. Root transform (movement, up-axis rotation)
2. Head transform (side-axis rotation, vertical look with **clamping typically ±90 degrees**)
3. Noise transform (displacement/shake only)
4. Camera transform (tilt override)

**Accessibility Approach:**
The design intentionally avoids realistic simulation in favor of playability, with toggle flags for tilt, shake, and displacement to prevent motion sickness.

**No Specific Numbers Provided:** The article omits precise milliseconds, frequencies, or amplitude values, instead emphasizing systemic design principles.

I found the research paper you're looking for. The Stahl (1999) study had normal human subjects make horizontal, centrifugal saccades along an encircling array of light-emitting diodes, with measurements of gaze, head, and eye-in-head angle made at the conclusion of the head movement or at the end of the eye movement in eye-only saccades.

Key findings from the study include:

The plots of head movement amplitude versus predicted eye eccentricity had a central flat region in which gaze shifts were unaccompanied by head movements (the eye-only range) and two flanking lobes in which head movement amplitude was a linear function of predicted eye eccentricity (the eye-head ranges).

The study was published in Experimental Brain Research in May 1999, volume 126(1):41-54.

Links: [{"title":"The human horizontal vestibulo-ocular reflex during combined linear and angular acceleration | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/PL00005639"},{"title":"The human vertical vestibulo-ocular reflex during combined linear and angular acceleration with near-target fixation | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00227649"},{"title":"Vestibular and non-vestibular contributions to eye movements that compensate for head rotations during viewing of near targets | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00221-005-2305-y"},{"title":"Human Horizontal Vestibulo-Ocular Reflex Initiation: Effects of Acceleration, Target Distance, and Unilateral Deafferentation | Journal of Neurophysiology | American Physiological Society","url":"https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/jn.1998.80.3.1151"},{"title":"Initial vestibulo-ocular reflex during transient angular and linear acceleration in human cerebellar dysfunction | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s002219900266"},{"title":"Temporal Dynamics of Ocular Position Dependence of the Initial Human Vestibulo-ocular Reflex - PMC","url":"https://ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC1866306"},{"title":"The human horizontal vestibulo-ocular reflex during combined linear and angular acceleration - PubMed","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9166920/"}]

Based on the search results, I found research by Viirre, Crane, and Demer on the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) with eye position and target distance effects.

Rotations were about a series of five axes ranging from 20 cm posterior to the eyes to 10 cm anterior to the eyes. In their study, subjects were asked to regard visible or remembered targets 10 cm, 25 cm, and 600 cm distant from the right eye.

The researchers employed binocular magnetic search coils to study the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) and visually enhanced vestibulo-ocular reflex (VVOR) of 15 human subjects undergoing passive, whole-body rotations about a vertical (yaw) axis.

Key findings from this work include: During sinusoidal rotations, the gain and phase of the VOR and VVOR were found to be highly dependent on target distance and eccentricity of the rotational axis. Additionally, rotation about axes anterior to the otoliths decreased gain, whereas rotation about axes posterior to the otoliths increased gain.

I found the seminal 1997 paper by Goossens and Van Opstal on human eye-head coordination. Here are the search results:

The paper "Human eye-head coordination in two dimensions under different sensorimotor conditions" was published by H. H. L. M. Goossens and A. John Van Opstal in May 1997 in Experimental Brain Research, volume 114, issue 3, pages 542–560.

The study investigated head-free gaze saccades of human subjects towards visual as well as auditory stimuli presented in the two-dimensional frontal plane, under both aligned and unaligned initial fixation conditions. Although the basic patterns for eye and head movements were qualitatively comparable for both stimulus modalities, systematic differences were obtained under aligned conditions, suggesting a task-dependent movement strategy.

The findings suggest an independent control of the eye and head motor systems by commands in different frames of reference.

This paper has become highly influential in the field and is widely cited in research on eye-head coordination, particularly regarding gaze shifts and the neural mechanisms underlying coordinated oculomotor control.

Based on the search results, here are the key findings related to your query:

## Head Movement Continuing After Gaze Shift

Once gaze has reached the target, it is stabilized at this position while the head continues to move. The eyes reach the target more quickly and must then be stabilized while the head continues to move.

## Eye Counter-Rotation and VOR Compensation

This stabilization, which is due to a counter-rotation of the eyes, is mediated by the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR). More specifically, the eyes generally begin to reverse direction at the end of the saccade during the counter-rotation phase, compensating for ongoing head movement.

## Head Undershoot

The search results mention head undershoots in the context of motor control. When an unchanged motor command drives a simulated weighted head plant the predicted head velocity trajectory shows oscillatory undershoots.

## Post-Saccadic Timing

Regarding the temporal dynamics, average gaze stability was comparable to controls 40 ms after the end of the eye saccade. Additionally, during gaze shifts, the VOR is inhibited until the end of the saccade, after which it reactivates to ensure post-saccadic gaze stabilization.

The search results provide good information on the coordination mechanisms, but don't contain specific data on the exact duration of post-saccadic head movement in milliseconds for all conditions you inquired about.

The Science Behind the Speed - ScienceInsights","url":"https://scienceinsights.org/how-fast-is-a-blink-the-science-behind-the-speed/"}]

Based on the search results, here's comprehensive information about blink kinematics:

## Overall Blink Duration

A spontaneous human blink typically lasts between 100 to 400 milliseconds, measured from the beginning of eyelid closure to when the lid is fully re-opened. The average spontaneous blink is around 100 to 150 milliseconds.

## Blink Phases

A typical blink is divided into three phases: closure, a brief period of being fully closed, and reopening. The phases have asymmetrical timing:

- **Down Phase (Closure)**: The initial closing movement often takes just 30 to 50 milliseconds.
- **Up Phase (Opening)**: The up phase is generally slower because the levator palpebrae muscle must overcome passive orbital forces, resulting in up phases that last longer than the down phase but move the eyelid the same amplitude.

## Blink Types

There are several types of blinks including reflexive blinks, which occur unconsciously in response to an outside stimulus; voluntary blinks, which occur consciously; and spontaneous blinks, which occur unconsciously and are not typically evoked in response to stimuli.

## Voluntary vs. Spontaneous Blinks

Voluntary blinks have a mean lid-closure duration of 13.4 ± 4.7 milliseconds, which was 61.4% longer than spontaneous blinks. Additionally, voluntary blinks tend to be slower and more prolonged than the unconscious, spontaneous ones.

I found relevant search results covering camera lag, smoothing, and damping techniques across Unity and Unreal. Here's what the results show:

## Key Findings:

**Camera Damping & Smoothing:**
Every Cinemachine Body component (Transposer, Framing Transposer, Orbital Transposer) has damping values that control how quickly the camera follows the target, with X, Y, and Z Damping fields in the Inspector. Zero damping means the camera teleports to the exact offset position every frame, causing micro-movements to mirror instantly, which reads as vibration, and even sub-pixel jitter becomes visible camera shake at zero damping.

**Damping Values:**
Start with damping values between 0.5 and 2.0 for the Body and between 1.0 and 3.0 for the Aim.

**First-Person Camera Lag:**
An advanced Unreal Engine first-person camera system includes headbobbing based on player velocity, camera dipping, tilting, depth of field, idle camera motion, camera pitch clamping, smooth camera lag, wall climbing, stamina and health system and rapid fall shaking.

**Spring Arm Component (Unreal):**
Spring Arm Component is a solution for cameras to expand/retract based on gameplay situations, typically when adding a Camera to a character for third person perspective, and automatically controls how the camera handles obstruction by level geometry.

**Unreal to Unity Migration:**
Unreal's Character Controller class and Spring Arm can be used for player control and camera switching achieved by switching parameters and Lerping between values, and this can be done similarly in Unity using Cinemachine.

I found the study you're looking for! The paper is titled "Not looking while leaping: the linkage of blinking and saccadic gaze shifts" by Evinger C, Manning KA, Pellegrini JJ, Basso MA, Powers AS, Sibony PA, published in Experimental Brain Research in 1994, volume 100(2):337-44.

Regarding your specific query about the findings: The study found that activation of the orbicularis oculi (the lid closing muscle) EMG occurred with 97% of saccadic gaze shifts larger than 33°. The blinks typically began simultaneously with the initiation of head and/or eye movement.

The study also examined what happens when subjects made saccadic head turns with their eyes closed. In this condition, orbicularis oculi EMG activity occurred with all head turns greater than 17° in amplitude and the EMG activity began an average of 39.3 ms before the start of the head movement.

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about the Zangemeister and Stark 1981 study on head movement dynamics.

## Search Results

Zangemeister, Jones, and Stark published "Dynamics of head movement trajectories: Main sequence relationship" in 1981. The article was published in Experimental Neurology in January 1981 (volume 71, issue 1, pages 76-91) with authors W H Zangemeister, A Jones, and L Stark.

Head movements, mainly rapid rotations of the skull in the horizontal plane, were measured and dynamic parameters of the trajectories displayed in "main sequence" diagrams. Basic neurological control patterns and several sources of variability were studied and compared with earlier results in the literature and with patterns of control of eye and arm rotation.

The "main sequence" concept is central to this work: The main sequence describes the systematic scaling of peak velocity with movement amplitude, reflecting biomechanical and neural constraints on motor execution.

The PubMed record (PMID: 7449898) is available, though no abstract is available on PubMed. The full article can be accessed through ScienceDirect or other academic databases using the DOI: 10.1016/0014-4886(81)90072-8.

Based on the search results, here are the key findings related to head yaw rotation axis location:

## Head Yaw Rotation Axis

The pivot point for head oscillations was close to the midpoint of the interaural line. This suggests that the yaw rotation axis is located near the interaural axis—the line connecting the two ears.

## Anatomical Basis

C2, the axis, articulates superiorly with C1 via a unique bony structure called the dens or odontoid process. The dens projects up from the vertebral body and articulates with the atlas. The dens permits pivoting motion and allows a greater range of motion in rotating the head laterally.

Additionally, the head interface with the dens and occipital condyles during head oscillation appears to be the physiological basis for head rotation during oscillatory movements.

However, the search results don't provide specific centimeter measurements for the distance from the eye centers to the axis of rotation or detailed anthropometric measurements relating to the eye-to-rotation-axis distance. If you need more precise quantitative anthropometric data, you may want to consult specialized biomechanics or ophthalmology literature on head tracking and gaze control systems.

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about Zangemeister and Stark's work on head movement types and the triphasic EMG pattern:

Fast as possible (time optimal) single joint movements throughout the body are characterized by the triphasic (3 pulse) pattern of activation in the agonist and antagonist muscles.

Zangemeister and Stark (1982) studied types of gaze movement involving variable interaction of eye and head movements. Their work appears to have been foundational in establishing the connection between time-optimal head movements and triphasic EMG patterns.

Control signals for time-optimal (with triphasic EMG activity) as well as suboptimal horizontal head rotations were obtained by means of inverse modeling procedures. The research showed how these control signals relate to the neurological mechanisms driving head movements.

The triphasic pattern itself comprises three phases: an agonist burst (AG1) initiates and accelerates the movement toward the target, antagonist activation (ANT) halts the movement at the exact target, and a second agonist burst (AG2) then reduces the effect of ANT to accurately place the limb at the predetermined endpoint of movement.

Based on the web search results, I found the seminal study you're looking for:

Guitton and Volle's 1987 study "Gaze control in humans: eye-head coordination during orienting movements to targets within and beyond the oculomotor range" was published in the Journal of Neurophysiology, volume 58, issue 3, pages 427-459.

The research demonstrated that in normal human subjects, rapid horizontal gaze shifts to visible and remembered targets situated within and beyond the oculomotor range (OMR) at offsets ranging from 30 to 160 degrees could be accomplished with the classic single-eye saccade and an accompanying saccadelike head movement.

The study defined gaze as the direction of the visual axis in space, which is the sum of the eye position relative to the head plus head position relative to space.

As the gaze movement amplitude increases, the amplitude of the eye saccade increases until saturation, and the contribution of the head becomes more prominent. This eye-head coordination pattern varies quantitatively between species as a function of the possible range of eye movements in the orbit, but its qualitative features remain constant across species.

This landmark paper continues to be widely cited in research on eye-head coordination and gaze control systems.

Based on your search query, here are the key findings about POV first-person film cinematography and head-mounted camera rigs:

## Hardcore Henry's Camera Rig Technique

Filmmaker Ilya Naishuller settled on a custom-made rig that positions two GoPro Hero 3 cameras on a helmet worn by the camera operator, who would perform in the action on-set. The rig was dubbed the "Adventure Mask."

The rig is comprised of a 3-axis Gimbal mount with a built-in stabilization system. The Hardcore Henry crew built and destroyed more than 30 prototypes, tried all the existing types of plastic, and made hundreds of changes to the design during the production process.

## Camera Placement Strategy

Valyaev saw that the viewer would be better immersed and could identify the protagonist if the camera was fixed on the protagonist's mouth. The film uses a head-mounted GoPro rig with two lenses located just beneath the nose of the operator (typically a stunt man).

## Stabilization Approach

Director Naishuller claims "We did HUNDREDS of tests for stabilization using our Go-Pro rigs. HARDCORE gently eases the audience into the POV style at the start of the film … so by the time the frantic scenes come on, the viewer has been comfortably 'living' in the shooting style for some time."

The search results provide comprehensive information about the camera techniques used in Hardcore Henry, though the results don't specifically include detailed information about "Lady in the Lake" or broader GoPro stabilization techniques beyond what was developed for Hardcore Henry.

"A sixth order nonlinear model for horizontal head rotations in humans is presented and investigated using experimental results on head movement trajectories and neck muscle EMG. The controller signals, structured in accordance with time optimal control theory, are parameterized, and controller signal parameter variations show a dominating influence on different aspects of the head movement trajectory. The model fits the common head acceleration types over a wide range of amplitudes, and also less common (dynamic overshoot) trajectories."
