# Abstract Text

"The coordination between eye and head movements during a rapid orienting gaze shift has been investigated mainly when subjects made horizontal movements towards visual targets with the eyes starting at the centre of the orbit. Under these conditions, it is difficult to identify the signals driving the two motor systems, because their initial motor errors are identical and equal to the coordinates of the sensory stimulus (i.e. retinal error). In this paper, we investigate head-free gaze saccades of human subjects towards visual as well as auditory stimuli presented in the two-dimensional frontal plane, under both aligned and unaligned initial fixation conditions. Although the basic patterns for eye and head movements were qualitatively comparable for both stimulus modalities, systematic differences were also obtained under aligned conditions, suggesting a task-dependent movement strategy. Auditory-evoked gaze shifts were endowed with smaller eye-head latency differences, consistently larger head movements and smaller concomitant ocular saccades than visually triggered movements. By testing gaze control for eccentric initial eye positions, we found that the head displacement vector was best related to the initial head motor-error (target-re-head), rather than to the initial gaze error (target-re-eye), regardless of target modality. These findings suggest an independent control of the eye and head motor systems by commands in different frames of reference. However, we also observed a systematic influence of the oculomotor response on the properties of the evoked head movements, indicating a subtle coupling between the two systems. The results are discussed in view of current eye-head coordination models."

## user

Tool result:
"Human saccades may or may not be associated with head movements. To date, little attention has been devoted to the mechanisms determining head movement recruitment and scaling. Normal human subjects made horizontal, centrifugal saccades along an encircling array of light-emitting diodes. Measurements of gaze, head, and eye-in-head angle were made at the conclusion of the head movement (or at the end of the eye movement in eye-only saccades). We found that head movement amplitude (deltaH) related in a simple fashion to the eye eccentricity that would have resulted if the gaze shift had been performed without a head movement. Plots of deltaH vs this predicted eye eccentricity (E(PRED)) had a central flat region in which gaze shifts were unaccompanied by head movements (the eye-only range) and two flanking lobes in which deltaH was a linear function of E(PRED) (the eye-head ranges). DeltaH correlated with EPRED better than with gaze shift amplitude, as would be expected if head movements were controlled so as to keep eye eccentricity within a particular range. Head movement tendencies were quantified by the width of the eye-only range, the slope of the eye-head range, and the width of the region within which the eye was likely to be found at the conclusion of the completed gaze-shifting behavior (the customary ocular motor range). The measures ranged widely in these normal subjects: 35.8+/-31.9 degrees for the eye-only range (mean+/-SD), 0.77+/-0.16 for the slope of the eye-head range, and 44.0+/-23.8 degrees for the customary ocular motor range. Yet for a given subject, the measurements were reproducible across experimental sessions, with the customary ocular motor range being the most consistent measure of the three. The form of the deltaH vs E(PRED) plots suggests that the neural circuitry underlying eye-head coordination carries out two distinct functions--gating the head movement and scaling the head movement. The reason for the large intersubject variability of head movement tendencies is unknown. It does not parallel intersubject differences in full-scale eye (in orbit) range or full-scale neck range."

Here is the abstractText field verbatim:

Gaze, the direction of the visual axis in space, is the sum of the eye position relative to the head (E) plus head position relative to space (H). In the old explanation, which we call the oculocentric motor strategy, of how a rapid orienting gaze shift is controlled, it is assumed that 1) a saccadic eye movement is programmed with an amplitude equal to the target's offset angle, 2) this eye movement is programmed without reference to whether a head movement is planned, 3) if the head turns simultaneously the saccade is reduced in size by an amount equal to the head's contribution, and 4) the saccade is attenuated by the vestibuloocular reflex (VOR) slow phase. Humans have an oculomotor range (OMR) of about +/- 55 degrees. The use of the oculocentric motor strategy to acquire targets lying beyond the OMR requires programming saccades that cannot be made physically. We have studied in normal human subjects rapid horizontal gaze shifts to visible and remembered targets situated within and beyond the OMR at offsets ranging from 30 to 160 degrees. Heads were attached to an apparatus that permitted short unexpected perturbations of the head trajectory. The acceleration and deceleration phases of the head perturbation could be timed to occur at different points in the eye movement. 4. Single-step rapid gaze shifts of all sizes up to at least 160 degrees (the limit studied) could be accomplished with the classic single-eye saccade and an accompanying saccadelike head movement. In gaze shifts less than approximately 45 degrees, when head motion was prevented totally by the brake, the eye attained the target. For larger target eccentricities the gaze shift was interrupted by the brake and the average eye saccade amplitude was approximately 45 degrees, well short of the OMR. Thus saccadic eye movement amplitude was neurally, not mechanically, limited. When the head's motion was not perturbed by the brake, the eye saccade amplitude was a function of head velocity: for a given target offset, the faster the head the smaller the saccade. For gaze shifts to targets beyond the OMR and when head velocity was low, the eye frequently attained the 45 degrees position limit and remained there, immobile, until gaze attained the target.(ABSTRACT TRUNCATED AT 400 WORDS)

Here is the abstractText field verbatim:

Static head orientations obey Donders' law and are postulated to be rotations constrained by a Fick gimbal. Head oscillations can be voluntary or generated during natural locomotion. Whether the rotation axes of the voluntary oscillations or during locomotion are constrained by the same gimbal is unknown and is the subject of this study. Head orientation was monitored with an Optotrak (Northern Digital). Human subjects viewed visual targets wearing pin-hole goggles to achieve static head positions with the eyes centered in the orbit. Incremental rotation axes were determined for pitch and yaw by computing the velocity vectors during head oscillation and during locomotion at 1.5 m/s on a treadmill. Static head orientation could be described by a generalization of the Fick gimbal by having the axis of the second rotation rotate by a fraction, k, of the angle of the first rotation without a third rotation. We have designated this as a k-gimbal system. Incremental rotation axes for both pitch and yaw oscillations were functions of the pitch but not the yaw head positions. The pivot point for head oscillations was close to the midpoint of the interaural line. During locomotion, however, the pivot point was considerably lower. These findings are well explained by an implementation of the k-gimbal model, which has a rotation axis superimposed on a Fick-gimbal system. This could be realized physiologically by the head interface with the dens and occipital condyles during head oscillation with a contribution of the lower spine to pitch during locomotion.

## user

Tool result:
# Research Article Summary

**Authors & Year:** Evinger C, Manning KA, Pellegrini JJ, Basso MA, Powers AS, Sibony PA (1994)

**Abstract:**

"Many vertebrates generate blinks as a component of saccadic gaze shifts. We investigated the nature of this linkage between saccades and blinking in normal humans. Activation of the orbicularis oculi, the lid closing muscle, EMG occurred with 97% of saccadic gaze shifts larger than 33 degrees. The blinks typically began simultaneously with the initiation of head and/or eye movement. To minimize the possibility that the blinks accompanying saccadic gaze shifts were reflex blinks evoked by the wind rushing across the cornea and eye-lashes as the head and eyes turned, the subjects made saccadic head turns with their eyes closed. In this condition, orbicularis oculi EMG activity occurred with all head turns greater than 17 degrees in amplitude and the EMG activity began an average of 39.3 ms before the start of the head movement. Thus, one component of the command for large saccadic gaze shifts appears to be a blink. We call these blinks gaze-evoked blinks. The linkage between saccadic gaze shifts and blinking is reciprocal. Evoking a reflex blink prior to initiating a voluntary saccadic gaze shift dramatically reduces the latency of the initiation of the head movement."

## user

Tool result:
# Publication Details

**Authors:** Fuller JH

**Year:** 1992

**Abstract:**

"In studies of human eye-head coordinated saccadic gaze shifts, different laboratories have found greatly different magnitudes of head movements for a given amplitude of gaze shift (head movement gain). The present study was conducted to examine why, and to quantify volitional head movements. Fixation/target lights were located at 20 degrees and 40 degrees on either side of a central light. There were two tasks or modes. In the non-aligned mode, gaze fixation (first light) was followed by a gaze step to the target (second light) accurately and quickly. In the head-aligned mode, the head was aligned within 3 degrees of the first fixation light (i.e., initial starting position) before the step sequence began. In both non-aligned and head-aligned modes, subject instructions pertaining to the second target light concerned only gaze; there was no requisite head position. The head movement propensity of nine subjects was ranked according to the mean gain (head/target amplitude) of two 40 degree jumps (0-40 degrees and -20 to +20 degrees) in the non-aligned mode. This ranking method clearly identified extreme head-movers and non-movers. The moderate movers were further characterized by three additional criteria, derived by comparing the gains in different jumps, which varied in starting position and amplitude. First, when the two 40 degree jumps were compared, typically the gain of non-movers was less in the symmetric jump (-20 to +20 degrees) with the gain of the head-movers was greater in the symmetric jump. Second, in the head-aligned mode the gain of non-movers progressively increased when the starting position was progressively moved eccentrically, whereas the gain of head-movers increased only slightly, if at all. Third, when the gains of two symmetric (40 degrees and 80 degrees) jumps were compared, the head-movers consistently had opposite trends from non-movers. These three comparative criteria and the initial criterion together define head movement propensity. To explain the above observations, three effects are proposed. First, a "midline-attraction" effect causes resistance to movement away from the midline in non-movers and an increase in movement amplitude if the jump starts eccentrically. Second, a "resetting" effect occurs when the eccentricity of the jump is varied; the stopping position is reset closer to the target. Third, an "awareness/arousal" effect increases the gain in the head-aligned mode due to the intrinsic nature of the alignment procedure."

# Key Quantitative Findings on Blink-Head Movement Coupling

## Fraction of Blinks with Head Movements
The paper does not explicitly state what fraction of blinks coincide with head movements. Rather, it reports that "blink probability increased with higher head velocities" across all movements studied.

## Timing of Blink Onset Relative to Head Movement
"Blink rate decreased beginning around 500 ms before head movement onset, reached a minimum shortly before head movement onset, and then peaked during the head movement."

The planning horizon differed by task: "Blocks: M=−531.27 ms, SD=138.21 ms; Paint: M=−384.57 ms, SD=124.95 ms"

## Blink Duration
The paper specifies: "gaze position and velocity vectors during the blinks (+/− 5 samples or 25 ms around the blinks) to NaN" but does not report typical blink duration as a primary measurement.

## Head Movement Velocity Threshold
The relationship was described as following "a cumulative Gaussian, akin to a typical psychometric function." Individual P50 points (velocity producing 50% blink probability) varied considerably across participants.

## Measurement Methods
Eye and head tracking used "mobile eye tracking glasses (Pupil Labs Neon)" with "IMU data to track head movements." Gaze was computed in world coordinates by combining eye position and head orientation data.

## user

Tool result:
# Head Movement During Gaze Shifts: Quantitative Findings

## Head Contribution as Fraction of Gaze Amplitude

**At 30°:** Head contributes approximately one-third of displacement (Saeb et al., 2011).

**At 40°:** Fuller (1992) classified participants by Head Gain ratio—head movers showed gains close to 1.0, while non-head movers scored below 0.3 at this amplitude.

**At 50°:** The dataset analysis revealed substantial variation, with low-contribution participants reaching ~30° head rotation and high-contribution participants approaching 45° at this eccentricity.

## Eye-Only Range and Head-Movement Thresholds

Stahl (1999) defined the Eye-Only Range (EOR) as "the range of target eccentricities relative to the head where gaze shifts are primarily accomplished by eye movements," operationally set where eye-only saccade probability exceeds 50%.

An eye-only saccade was defined as one where "head movement does not exceed 10% of the shift" (Oommen & Stahl, 2005; Thumser et al., 2008).

Small horizontal shifts below 20° typically involve "almost entirely the eyes," while larger shifts increasingly recruit head movement (Land & Tatler, 2009).

## Population Distribution

The study identified a **continuous spectrum** rather than discrete categories. Most participants clustered in intermediate ranges, with:
- Eye-mover extreme: PC1 score of –49.30
- Head-mover extreme: PC1 score of +38.28
- ~10° consistent separation between spectrum ends after 20° eccentricity

## Individual Variation Magnitude

At 20° eccentricity: head contribution ranged from "only around 5° at the low end to nearly 15° at the high end—a **threefold difference**."

Task-dependent consistency: r = 0.60 correlation between abstract and video tasks, indicating "moderate alignment" across contexts while allowing contextual modulation.

# First-Person Camera Motion Advice Summary

Based on the Blender Artists Community discussion, the primary recommendations for realistic first-person camera motion include:

**Main Techniques:**
- Parent the camera to an armature's head bone, then adjust intensity through constraint influence or keyframe scaling
- Use a Copy Location Constraint on the Z-axis alone with reduced influence to control vertical bobbing separately
- Bake camera keyframes and modify animation curves for fine-tuning

**Refinement Methods:**
- Apply noise to animation curves to add "hecticness" and realism
- Create sharp slopes in curves occasionally to break up smoothness and enhance the first-person feel
- Balance readability with movement intensity—avoid both excessive bobbing and over-smoothing

**Tools Mentioned:**
- Camera_shakify addon (GitHub resource) with preset motion patterns
- Blender's native noise modifier and animation curve editor

**Note:** The discussion does not address pivot placement, motion capture, overshoot, or roll. No specific numerical values are provided for adjusting motion parameters.

Tool result:
# Blink Kinematics: Extracted Numbers and Citations

## Blink Rate
"They typically occur unconsciously, roughly 15 times per minute" (Riggs et al., 1981; Volkmann et al., 1982; Evinger, 1995; Ousler et al., 2014; Van Opstal et al., 2016)

## Total Blink Duration
"From inception to completion, blinks operate on the order of hundreds of milliseconds" and "the full process of a blink can last 250–450 milliseconds" (Volkmann et al., 1982)

## Down-Phase (Lid Closing) Duration
"The down phase lasts between 50 and 130 ms" (Stern et al., 1984). Additionally, "lasting roughly 75–100 milliseconds" describes typical down-phase timing (Evinger et al., 1991).

## Full Lid Closure Duration
"Full lid closure can last 10–50 milliseconds" (Ousler et al., 2014), though longer durations occur with voluntary blinks or dry eyes.

## Overall Occlusion Duration
"Occlusion lasts ∼40–200 ms" (Riggs et al., 1981; Volkmann et al., 1982)

## Visual Suppression Timeline
"Visual sensitivity to changes in luminance started decreasing prior to a voluntary blink, by about 100 milliseconds, and did not return to baseline levels until approximately 200 milliseconds after the blink onset" (Volkmann et al., 1980)

## Reflex vs. Voluntary vs. Spontaneous Differences
"The down phase of reflex blinks is the fastest, while voluntary blinks are slower and more similar to spontaneous blinks which are the slowest" (Evinger et al., 1991)

# HEAD KINEMATICS IN HUMANS: Key Numerical Values

## Head Peak Velocity vs. Gaze Amplitude
The study found that **peak head-in-space velocity increased significantly with target eccentricity** (90°, 135°, 180°), though specific regression values comparing head velocity to gaze amplitude were not explicitly stated for those relationships.

For **single-step vs. multiple-step transfers**, the authors reported:
- "peak velocity was significantly lower during multiple-step than during single-step gaze shifts (172 ± 52 vs. 279 ± 80, 211 ± 64 vs. 363 ± 110 and 266 ± 54 vs. 428 ± 127°/s to targets of 90°, 135° and 180° eccentricity"

## Head Movement Duration
"Head-in-space peak velocity (Fig. 3a) and duration increased thereby as a function of target eccentricity (factorial ANOVA, F = 26.1 p < 0.0001 and F = 25.1 p < 0.0001)"

## Head Onset Latency Relative to Eye
The document emphasizes that **head onset timing relative to eye movement showed complex patterns** depending on target predictability rather than providing fixed latency values.

## Time from Gaze End to Head End
**Peak head-in-space velocity preceded gaze shift termination**: "peak head-in-space velocity does not coincide with gaze shift end; it occurs earlier and earlier as gaze shift duration and amplitude increase"

Links: [{"title":"Behavior of the human translational vestibulo-ocular reflex during simultaneous head translation and rotation - PubMed","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25564074/"},{"title":"vertical translation vestibulo-ocular: Topics by Science.gov","url":"https://www.science.gov/topicpages/v/vertical+translation+vestibulo-ocular"},{"title":"Ocular Reflex Phase during Off-Vertical Axis Rotation in Humans is Modified by Head-Turn-On-Trunk Position | Scientific Reports","url":"https://www.nature.com/articles/srep42071"},{"title":"translational vestibulo-ocular reflex: Topics by Science.gov","url":"https://www.science.gov/topicpages/t/translational+vestibulo-ocular+reflex"},{"title":"Influence of eye and head position on the vestibulo-ocular reflex | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00238215"},{"title":"The Cerebellar Nodulus/Uvula Integrates Otolith Signals for the Translational Vestibulo-Ocular Reflex","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2981566/"}]

I found search results related to the vestibulo-ocular reflex and head rotation. Here are the key findings:

During eccentric rotation with the head displaced approximately 10 cm anterior, subjects were rotated at 1 Hz while viewing a near target, and were also translated along the inter-aural axis at 1.9 Hz. During eccentric rotation, the compensation ratio was 0.93, indicating strong compensation for eye movements during off-axis head rotation.

Head movements in the sagittal pitch plane typically involve off-axis rotation requiring both vertical and horizontal vergence ocular reflexes to compensate for angular and translational motion relative to visual targets of interest.

The otolith-driven translational vestibulo-ocular reflex (tVOR) generates compensatory eye movements to linear head accelerations. Studies in humans with degenerative diseases suggest the cerebellum plays a role, since their response to interaural (lateral) translation is often dramatically attenuated.

The research indicates that the vestibular system compensates for both rotational and translational head movements, with particular importance placed on maintaining visual stability during off-axis rotation where the eyes are displaced anterior to the axis of rotation.

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about these anthropometric measurements:

**Key Definitions:**
Tragion is the superior point on the juncture of the cartilaginous flap (tragus) of the ear with the head, and ectocanthus is the external corner of the eye.

**Head Sagittal Plane Measurements:**
The measurement between ectocanthion and the vertical plane tangent to the back of the head is documented as a standard anthropometric measurement. Similarly, the occiput to tragion distance is the horizontal distance between tragion and the vertical plane tangent to the back of the head.

**Ear Canal Distance (Sagittal Plane):**
For measurements related to ear canal distances on the sagittal plane, the distance between the projection point of the center of the sound outlet on the sagittal plane and the centroid of the projection of the ear canal opening on the sagittal plane is in a range of 2.2 mm to 3.8 mm.

The search results provide anatomical definitions and some specific measurements related to the sagittal plane and ear canal positioning, though they don't include comprehensive standardized anthropometric values for all the specific combinations you're asking about.

# First-Person View Type Parameters Affecting Look/Turn Feel

Based on the documentation, here are the parameters that influence first-person camera responsiveness:

## Primary Motion Control
- **Position Spring & Rotation Spring**: Handle regular responsive motion (stiffness/damping values not specified)
- **Field Of View & Field Of View Damping**: Control framing and transition speed (default values not provided)
- **Pitch Limit**: Sets vertical look angle range (specific defaults omitted)

## Head Tracking
- **Smooth Head Offset Steps**: "averages recent humanoid head offsets. Set it to `0` to disable" head-position tracking
- **Rotate With Head**: Includes animated head rotation
- **Vertical Offset Lerp Speed**: Smooths vertical movement when head bone is absent

## Camera Motion Effects
- **Bob Positional & Roll Rates**: Set frequency; amplitudes control strength
- **Input Velocity Scale & Maximum Input Velocity**: Keep speed changes controlled
- **Rotation Strafe Roll**: Leans with sideways velocity
- **Secondary Rotation Speed**: Smooths override or gravity-alignment rotation

## Look Offset Configuration
- **Look Offset**: Positions view relative to character anchor
- **Look Down Offset**: Prevents camera entering body during downward pitch
- **Collision Radius**: Prevents clipping scene geometry

**Note**: The documentation does not provide specific default numerical values for most parameters.

## user

Tool result:
# Camera Rotation and Movement Rules in VR

Based on the provided guide, **no specific numerical limits are given** for rotation speed, acceleration rates, or timing measurements (deg/s, seconds, etc.).

The document provides only qualitative guidance:

**Rotation:**
- "Avoid rotational movements, as they can be particularly disorienting in VR"
- No maximum rotation speed or acceleration parameters specified

**Movement:**
- Prioritize "smooth, consistent motion" over abrupt changes
- "Forward movements are generally more comfortable for viewers than lateral or backward motions"
- Recommend "slow movements" when using stabilization tools
- Suggest allowing "a period of stillness to let viewers reorient themselves" after camera motion

**Stabilization:**
- Use gimbals to reduce jitter and maintain "a consistent frame"
- Apply post-production stabilization for "minor jitters"

**Notable:** The guide emphasizes practical best practices and production techniques rather than providing technical specifications with measurable thresholds. No information about pivot points (eye-level vs. neck-level) or roll specifications appears in this content.

I've completed the web search for your query about first-person camera neck pivot offset and eye rotation in VR applications. The search returned results related to VR camera implementation, head tracking, camera offsets, and eye positioning in various game engines and VR frameworks.

Here are the key findings:

**VR Camera Architecture & Tracking:**
The OVRCameraRig provides the Transform object representing headset tracking space, with CenterEyeAnchor as an anchor for the main camera, and separate RightEyeAnchor and LeftEyeAnchor anchors for each eye.

**First-Person Camera Positioning:**
The camera looks more accurate when positioned at the character's eyes, and a camera offset is precalculated from the avatar to place the camera roughly where the avatar's head is.

**Parallax & Head Rotation Effects:**
One user reported a specific issue where when turning their head left and right in SteamVR, they felt as if their eyes were slightly zooming in and out as their head rotated, describing it as a camera offset or FOV mismatch issue.

**Eye vs. Head Control:**
Some developers have explored controlling eyes directly rather than the head, with the head tracking to follow eye direction, which gives viewers different information and creates a different picture, with the eyes attached as children to the head so their 3D position is affected and rotation compensated for as the head rotates.

The search results cover the technical implementation aspects but don't contain specific numeric values (like the exact "cm" measurements you mentioned) for standard eye offsets or neck pivot distances.

## user

Tool result:
Based on the tutorial content provided, the document does **not** contain specific technical specifications for realistic POV camera movement such as eye-lead timing, frame counts, overshoot/settle measurements, ease curve recommendations, or pivot placement details.

The tutorial offers these general principles instead:

**Documented Techniques:**

1. **Subtle movement emphasis**: "Subtle movement: Both character and camera motion tend to stay subtle so the audience can easily understand what is going on."

2. **Gentle camera behavior**: "Subtle camera motion keeps the audience oriented and avoids dizziness or distraction, which is crucial."

3. **Eye-line positioning**: Keep "the camera near the character's eye line so that the perspective feels like a believable head height and orientation."

4. **Avoid fast, distracting moves**: "Avoid overly fast or distracting camera moves, which can pull the audience out of the shot."

5. **Small motivated adjustments**: The bike reference demonstrates "small, motivated camera changes rather than big, flashy moves."

6. **Asymmetrical hand poses**: Avoid "twinning in visible body parts such as hands or arms. Small asymmetries and varied poses create more organic, less stiff compositions."

The content emphasizes *principles* rather than measurable specifications. Marta recommends recording live-action reference to determine appropriate movement ranges for specific scenarios, but no concrete numbers, frame counts, or millisecond timing values are provided.

Links: [{"title":"Initial vestibulo-ocular reflex during transient angular and linear acceleration in human cerebellar dysfunction | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s002219900266"},{"title":"Behavior of the human translational vestibulo-ocular reflex during simultaneous head translation and rotation - PubMed","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25564074/"},{"title":"Influence of eye and head position on the vestibulo-ocular reflex | Experimental Brain Research | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00238215"},{"title":"Investigation on biomechanical responses in bilateral semicircular canals and nystagmus in vestibulo-ocular reflex experiments under different forward-leaning angles","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10879882/"},{"title":"Influence of eye and head position on the vestibulo-ocular reflex - PubMed","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3770110/"},{"title":"Hybrid model of the context dependent vestibulo-ocular reflex: implications for vergence-version interactions","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4321407/"},{"title":"Neuroanatomy, Vestibulo-ocular Reflex - StatPearls - NCBI Bookshelf","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK545297/"},{"title":"Somatosensory Influence on Platform-Induced Translational Vestibulo-Ocular Reflex in Vertical Direction in Humans","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7238866/"}]

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about eye location and head rotation axes in vestibulo-ocular reflex studies:

The axis of head rotation was varied so that it was located 20 cm behind the eyes, 7 cm behind the eyes (centered between the otoliths), centered between the eyes, or 10 cm anterior to the eyes. This comes from a study on vestibulo-ocular reflex during transient angular and linear acceleration.

Additionally, subjects were rotated with their head displaced approximately 10 cm anterior (eccentric rotation) in another vestibulo-ocular reflex study examining near target viewing. During eccentric rotation the compensation ratio was 0.93, indicating that the vestibulo-ocular reflex accounts for the geometric relationship between eye position and the rotation axis to maintain gaze on near targets.

These studies demonstrate how the vestibulo-ocular reflex compensates for different head rotation axes positioned at various distances relative to the eyes, particularly when fixating near targets.

directions, both upwards diagonal directions and both downwards diagonal directions. However,
differences were found between the two vertical directions. Therefore, the results presented in
this section have been categorised into upwards, downwards, horizontal, upwards diagonal and
downward diagonal gaze shifts for readability.

4.1 Temporal coupling and patterns of movement

For each gaze shift, we analysed the start- and end-time for associated eye, head and torso movement,
to identify general patterns of coordination and temporal relationships. The reaction time before
any movement was registered was on average 200ms for within-view targets, and 375ms for beyond-
view targets. The additional reaction time for beyond-view targets is explained by participants
needing to process the directional arrow.

   4.1.1 Patterns of movement. Fig. 4 illustrates the different gaze shifts that participants performed
during the study. The choice of movements during the gaze shift was dependent on multiple factors;
amplitude, direction, posture as well as individual differences. The eyes were generally first to
move toward a target, followed by optional head and torso movement. Head movement generally
preceded any torso movement.

   Smaller gaze shifts at amplitudes of 15� or less were generally performed by the eyes only, without
any significant head or torso movement. This corresponds with observations reported for gaze in
the real world [13, 16, 33]. Head movement that supported larger gaze shifts generally started before
the gaze target was reached, whereas any torso movement was further delayed, and frequently
only occurred after a gaze target was first reached.

   Both the head and torso would often continue to move or start moving after the target had
been reached by the eyes, to which the eyes responded with stabilising VOR movement in the
opposite direction. These movements were made by the participants to reach an eye-in-head
and head-in-torso position that is more comfortable over an extended period. We observed these
movement patterns across all amplitudes and postures in our VR study, and they correspond with
gaze behaviour observed in real-world studies [58, 73].

   4.1.2 Lag between eye, head and torso. For any gaze shifts supported by head movement, we
observed that the lag between eye and head differed depending on visibility of the target. For
within-view targets, participants started a head movement on average at 150ms after the start of
the eye movement, whereas the difference was only 30ms on average for beyond-view targets
(Fig. 5). These results seem to confirm previous research suggesting that there is an earlier head
movement when the gaze shift is the result of top-down goal-driven behaviour, as is the case for

Fig. 4. Patterns of eye, head and torso movement associated with gaze shifts. Gaze shifts start with eye movement. Head and torso can contribute  Ludwig Sidenmark and Hans Gellersen
toward reaching a target when the gaze shift is longer, and start or continue move after a target was reached. Any eye movement after a target
was reached is in the opposite direction to stabilise the eye on the target.
Eye, Head and Torso Coordination During Gaze Shifts in Virtual Reality  1:13

Fig. 5. Distribution of head movement start-times relative to the start of the eye movement, shown for the
different combinations of posture (left: sitting; right: standing) and target visibility (top: within-view; bottom:
beyond-view).

Fig. 6. Distribution of eventual torso movement start-times relative to start of the head movement. The torso
is used more and follows the head with less delay when users stand and the gaze shift involves a horizontal
component.

beyond-view targets, as opposed to a bottom-up reaction to a target appearing, which may be a
factor for within-view targets [10].

   The torso would rarely move independently and would mainly start after the head movement.
Torso movement was generally not used in conjunction with the gaze shift for amplitudes under
45�. Additionally, torso movement was rarely used in vertical directions or in sitting posture. If a
torso movement was used, then it was often used in a later period during the gaze shift, on average
550ms after the head movement. However, torso movement was found for 85% of all horizontal and

                ACM Transactions on Computer-Human Interaction, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article 1. Publication date: January 2019.
1:14  Ludwig Sidenmark and Hans Gellersen

Fig. 7. Median time to reach the target as a function of amplitude for each direction and posture, excluding
reaction time. The vertical line denotes the boundary between within-view and beyond-view gaze shifts. Gaze
shifts in the horizontal direction are fastest, and vertical gaze shifts are slowest.

diagonal shifts in the standing posture at amplitudes of 45� or larger, and in these cases followed
the head more quickly, with a lag of only 300ms on average (Fig. 6).

   4.1.3 Time to Reach Target. Figure 7 shows the median time to reach the target. For small
amplitudes, the reach time is quick since only the fast eyes are needed to reach the target. However,
the reach time increases for amplitudes larger than 35� due to that the slower head needs to be
used to be able to reach the target. We observed no HMD specific effect; once a gaze shift had been
initiated, there was no marked slowing down in reaching targets just beyond view, compared to
just within view.

   We also see a pattern in both postures where the vertical gaze shifts require a longer time to
reach the target compared to diagonal and horizontal gaze shifts. Horizontal gaze shifts were shown
to be the fastest. No large differences were found when comparing between postures. Also, note
that differences between directions mainly appear at the largest amplitudes (80� and 100�).

   4.1.4 Single Trial Examples. Figure 8 shows single-trial examples of typical gaze shifts performed
by participants during the study. The top row shows gaze to a target within-view at a amplitude of
35�. The target is reached in one ballistic movement, largely based on eye movement with some
support by the head. Upon reaching the target, head and eye movement become opposing and the
gaze is stabilised. The pattern is equivalent for sitting versus standing posture.

   The bottom row in Figure 8 shows a typical example of a larger gaze shift, to a target presented
beyond-view at a amplitude of 100�, exposing a strikingly different movement pattern. Large gaze
shifts would commonly start with the eyes and head moving towards the target. However, as the
eyes are much faster than the head, the eyes would move and then wait for the head to catch up
before making another movement. The eyes would in general stay closer to the head compared to
what we initially expected. We thought that the eyes would continuously stay ahead of the head
at larger eccentricity, gazing toward the edge of the HMD screen where the target is expected to
appear, whereas what we found is a distinct stepped behaviour of less eccentric eye shifts and VOR
eye movements until the gaze target is reached. This pattern starts to appear at amplitudes of 45�
and becomes more prevalent when the amplitude increases. We observed this behaviour in both
sitting and standing posture, for all gaze directions.

   The single-trial examples also illustrate the absence of torso movement for smaller gaze shifts
versus their contribution to larger shifts. Note that the head position shown in these plots represents
the combination of torso orientation and head-on-torso relative rotation.

Tool result:
4.3 Total movement of systems

As illustrated by the single-trial examples shown above (Fig. 8), much of the head and torso
movement associated with gaze shifts occurs after a target is first reached by the eyes. Our next
analysis thus considered the total movement of head and torso toward the gaze target. The results
are summarised in Figure 10, showing how far head and torso turned toward a target as a percentage
of the gaze amplitude.

   4.3.1 Total Head Movement. The results show that the relative total head movement was larger
than the head and torso contribution together, as participants generally continued to move their
head after the target was reached in order to obtain a more comfortable eye-in-head position. We
observed a high variance in the amount of head movement from participant to participant, with
differences of up 30� in how much they oriented their head toward a target at the end of gaze shift.
This indicates that different tendencies in using head movement, previously observed in real-world
studies, also show in VR.

   We further observed that the total head movement was generally short of the full gaze amplitude.
While participants often continued to rotate their head toward a target after first reaching it with
their eyes, their head did not follow their eyes all the way. This behaviour was found in both
postures and all directions. It can be explained by the relative effort required for head versus eye
movement, and relative saving of energy by only moving the head as far as reaching a comfortable
as opposed to central eye-in-head position.

   The total head movement was symmetrical in horizontal, upwards diagonal and downwards
diagonal directions but participants tended to move their head further upwards compared to
downwards. Statistical analysis showed that not only amplitude but also direction and posture
affected total head movement.

   Over shorter amplitudes within-view, head movement is not necessarily needed to achieve a
gaze shift, but given the short range it can be performed in any direction without strain. As such
the amount of head movement can be highly varied, and we observed relative head movement
from none to almost 90% of the gaze amplitude. However for beyond-view, head movement was
essential to bring the target into the FOV and we therefore saw less variation in the amount of
relative head movement. Direction had a main effect in both postures for beyond-view amplitudes
where more head movement was generally made in horizontal and diagonal directions. Posture
only had a significant main effect in horizontal and both diagonal directions where the extra torso
movement would help the head move further. Amplitude only showed a main effect for movements
with horizontal component in seated posture, where the relative total head movement peaks at
60� and then decreases as the torso is not able to help the head to travel further. For full statistical
analysis, see Appendix D.

   4.3.2 Total Torso Movement. Just as with the head, we saw torso movement continued or started
after a target was reached, but to a much smaller extent. Participants would generally only use

124 Pixel values obtained from the eye-tracking device were converted to visual angles based on the
125 device specifications (horizontal: 82�/1088 pixels; vertical: 82�/1080 pixels). The coordinate origin
126 of the scene camera was set at the center of the screen. Eye angular velocity was calculated by
127 differentiating position data, head angular velocity was obtained directly from the gyroscope, and
128 head position data were computed by integrating head velocity. Figure 2A shows a representative
129 example of mean positional waveforms during the task, and Figure 2B presents a representative
130 example of raw positional waveforms. In this study, following prior work, the onset of scanning
131 (Time 0 ms) was defined as the moment when horizontal head angular velocity exceeded 125 deg/s
132 (McGuckian et al., 2019). Data were segmented from -200 ms relative to this onset until the end of
133 each trial. As participants were instructed to align their head and eyes toward the ramp at trial onset,
134 head and eye positions at -200 ms were normalized to 0�. Furthermore, the "gaze-shift phase" was
135 defined as the interval from when gaze angular velocity exceeded 250 deg/s until it fell below this
136 threshold again. This threshold was selected as a conservative criterion for capturing dynamic,
137 match-like movements in this study, based on prior criteria for head movement (125 deg/s; Chalkley
138 et al., 2018; McGuckian et al., 2019), saccade velocity thresholds (120-150 deg/s; Gregori et al.,
139 2016; Kishita et al., 2020), and gaze-shift velocity thresholds (~240 deg/s; Parker et al., 2023;
140 Manakhov et al., 2024). This threshold was intended to robustly capture relatively slow head-driven
141 rotations as well as fast eye-driven movements. Analyses were restricted to horizontal (yaw-axis)
142 movements. Signals were low-pass filtered using a 4th-order Butterworth filter with a cutoff
143 frequency of 50 Hz. The computed metrics included peak velocity, mean velocity, and amplitude for
144 both eyes and head; coefficients of variation (CV); cross-correlations with gaze; and mean-velocity-
145 to-amplitude ratios, as illustrated in Figure 2.

146 2.5. Statistics

147 Paired t-tests or Wilcoxon signed-rank tests were used to compare the eye and head variables listed in
148 Section 2.4, as well as the time conditions (800 ms vs. 1600 ms). Effect sizes (Cohen's d or r) were
149 also calculated. Cross-correlation analyses were conducted to examine the relationships between gaze
150 and eye movements and between gaze and head movements (correlation coefficients reported as CC).
151 In addition, Pearson's correlation analyses were used to examine associations between two variables.
152 Because independent hypotheses were tested for each variable, no corrections for multiple
153 comparisons were applied. For all tests, the significance level was set at  = .05. Statistical analyses
154 were performed using IBM SPSS Statistics version 30 (SPSS Inc.). Unless otherwise noted, data are
155 presented as mean � standard deviation.

156 3 Results

157 3.1. Comparison between eye and head movements during gaze shifts

158 Figure 3A illustrates representative examples of peak velocity and amplitude for the eyes and head.
159 As shown in Figure 3B, paired t-tests revealed that peak velocity was significantly higher for the eyes
160 than for the head (t(19) = 18.31, p < .001, d = 4.09). Mean velocity (t(19) = 4.77, p < .001, d = 1.07)
161 and amplitude (t(19) = 5.97, p < .001, d = 1.34) were also significantly greater for the eyes than for
162 the head. Figure 4A presents representative waveforms of mean velocity for gaze, eyes, and head. As
163 shown in Figure 4B, cross-correlation analysis revealed that the maximum correlation coefficient
164 (CC) was 0.98 for gaze-eye and 0.90 for gaze-head, with Wilcoxon signed-rank tests indicating that
165 gaze-eye CC was significantly higher (Z = 3.92, p < .001, r = .88). These results indicate that gaze
166 shifts are led by the eyes, while the head plays a supportive role, as illustrated in Figures 3 and 4.

--- soccer 300-342 ---
236 programs. For example, head-dominant players may benefit from tasks that require rapid decision-
237 making with stabilized eye control, whereas eye-dominant players may benefit from broader
238 exploratory tasks that exploit the head's range of motion, enabling training tailored to individual
239 characteristics.

240 4.3. Eyes confer stability

241 This study demonstrated that the eye movements play a stabilizing role in gaze shifts. Our results
242 revealed that the coefficients of variation (CV) for peak velocity and amplitude were significantly
243 lower for the eye than for the head. The peak eye velocity of (565 � 49 deg/s) was close to the
244 saturation range of saccadic velocity (600-700 deg/s; Bahill et al., 1975), and the mean amplitude
245 (34.8 � 6.6�) was also near the upper limit of ocular range of motion (30-40�; Freedman, 2008).
246 Thus, the eye movements operated near their physiological limits, leading to suppressed variability in
247 velocity and amplitude and consequently to lower CV values. Moreover, because the eye movements
248 are less affected by inertia and antagonist muscle activity (Robinson, 1981) and function as effectors
249 that reliably execute superior colliculus (SC) commands, they exhibited more consistent movements
250 compared with the head. The possibility that stability of gaze control contributes to the accuracy of
251 decision-making has been noted in reviews of sports science (H�ttermann et al., 2018), and the
252 present findings provide experimental support for this view. Ocular stability may underpin accurate

                                                                                                                                            6
         bioRxiv preprint doi: https://doi.org/10.1101/2025.09.19.677484; this version posted September 20, 2025. The copyright holder for this preprint
                         (which was not certified by peer review) is the author/funder. All rights reserved. No reuse allowed without permission.

253 decision-making, as evidenced by reports that elite soccer players' superior visual function strongly
254 correlates with executive functions related to decision-making, such as selective attention and
255 cognitive flexibility (Kn�llner et al., 2022). Future research should directly examine the relationship
256 between ocular stability and actual decision-making accuracy, which may enable the application of
257 these insights to training practice.

258 4.4. The head adapts to environmental demands

259 Our findings indicated that the head plays a role in flexibly adapting to environmental conditions.
260 The results showed that peak head velocity was significantly higher in the 800 ms condition than in
261 the 1600 ms condition, whereas peak eye velocity did not differ between conditions. This result
262 reflects the fact that the eye movements, operating near their physiological limits, show limited
263 variability in velocity and amplitude, whereas the head possesses a greater capacity for adjustment
264 due to the cervical range of motion and muscular flexibility. Indeed, a previous study has shown that
265 peak head velocity continues to increase with amplitude without saturation (Zangemeister et al.,
266 1981), and the present findings are consistent with this. Thus, the findings suggest that under
267 temporal constraints, the contribution of the head increases. Furthermore, it has been reported that
268 temporal pressure alters exploratory behavior in sports contexts (Vater et al., 2020), which aligns
269 with the present results. From a practical perspective, manipulating time constraints may emphasize

{"command": "PY=\"C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/hyperframes-student-kit/.venv/Scripts/python.exe\"; \"$PY\" - <<'EOF'\nimport numpy as np\nd=np.deg2rad\ndef Rx(a): c,s=np.cos(a),np.sin(a); return np.array([[1,0,0],[0,c,-s],[0,s,c]])\ndef Ry(a): c,s=np.cos(a),np.sin(a); return np.array([[c,0,s],[0,1,0],[-s,0,c]])\ndef Rz(a): c,s=np.cos(a),np.sin(a); return np.array([[c,-s,0],[s,c,0],[0,0,1]])\n# frame: x right, y up, z forward (line of sight). yaw about y, pitch about x, roll about z.\ndef rotvec(R):\n    ang=np.arccos((np.trace(R)-1)/2)\n    n=np.array([R[2,1]-R[1,2],R[0,2]-R[2,0],R[1,0]-R[0,1]])/(2*np.sin(ang))\n    return np.tan(ang/2)*n, ang\nfor yaw,pitch in [(44,-4),(44,0),(20,-4),(44,-10)]:\n    Rf=Ry(d(yaw))@Rx(d(pitch))          # Fick: yaw about space-vertical, then pitch about nested axis\n    Rh=Rx(d(pitch))@Ry(d(yaw))          # Helmholtz: pitch first, then yaw about nested vertical\n    for name,R in ((\"Fick\",Rf),(\"Helmholtz\",Rh)):\n        r,ang=rotvec(R)\n        tors=np.rad2deg(2*np.arctan(r[2]))  # torsion = component about line-of-sight (z)\n        print(f\"yaw {yaw:3d} pitch {pitch:3d} {name:9s} rotvec-torsion = {tors:+.2f} deg\")\nprint()\n# eye translation for a pivot d cm behind the (cyclopean) eye\nfor dcm in (7.0,7.5,8.5,10.0):\n    for yaw in (20,44):\n        x=dcm*np.sin(d(yaw)); z=dcm*(1-np.cos(d(yaw)))\n        # parallax between monitor at 65 cm and wall at 250 cm from a 'x' cm lateral shift\n        par=np.rad2deg(np.arctan(x/65)-np.arctan(x/250))\n        print(f\"pivot {dcm:4.1f} cm behind eye, yaw {yaw:2d}: lateral {x:4.2f} cm, backward {z:4.2f} cm, monitor-vs-wall parallax {par:4.2f} deg\")\nprint()\n# motion-blur numbers: 53 deg hFOV over 1920 px -> px/deg ; per-frame motion at peak velocity\npxdeg=1920/53\nfor fps in (30,60):\n    for vpk in (150,200,250,300):\n        per=vpk/fps\n        print(f\"fps {fps} peak {vpk} deg/s: {per:5.2f} deg/frame = {per*pxdeg:5.0f} px/frame at 1080p ({per*pxdeg*2:5.0f} px at 4K); 180deg-shutter streak {per*pxdeg/2:4.0f} px\")\nprint()\n# minimum-jerk profile: duration T for amplitude A given peak velocity Vpk = 1.875*A/T\nfor A,V in ((44,180),(44,220),(44,260),(20,120)):\n    print(f\"A={A} deg, Vpk={V} deg/s -> min-jerk duration T = {1.875*A/V*1000:4.0f} ms; bell (sine) profile T = {np.pi/2*A/V*1000:4.0f} ms\")\nEOF", "description": "Compute Fick torsion, eye translation, motion-blur and duration numbers"}

Tool result:
# Human Head-Eye Movement Metrics from Search Results

Based on the abstracts provided, only **one study reports specific numeric values** for head movement parameters in humans:

**Cho et al., 2026** (Capacity Versus Performance of Volitional Head Turns After Mild Traumatic Brain Injury):
- "Individuals with mTBI demonstrated slower in-laboratory head turns than controls (223.53 (62.32) deg/s versus 302.01 (55.88) deg/s; P = 0.006)"

**Note:** While several abstracts discuss head and eye movements (Ries et al. 2025; Laborde et al. 2025; Bayer & Zimmermann 2024; Shuai et al. 2025; Mavroudis et al. 2026), they do not include specific numeric values for peak velocity, duration, or latency measurements in their abstract text. The Verdone et al. 2026 study on mice explicitly excludes human data per the query parameters.

"For the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) to function properly, namely to ensure a stable retinal image under all circumstances, it should be able to take into account varying eye positions in the orbit and varying orientations of the head with respect to the axis about which it is rotating. We tested this capability by quantifying the gain and the time constant of the horizontal component of the VOR during rotation about an earth vertical axis when the line of sight (optical axis) was moved out of the plane of head rotation--either by rotating the eyes up or down in the orbit or by pitching the head up or down with respect to earth-horizontal. In either case the gain of the horizontal component of the VOR was attenuated precisely by the cosine of the angle made between the optical axis and the plane of head rotation. Furthermore, if the head was pitched up or down but the eye rotated oppositely in the orbit so as to keep the line of sight in the plane of head rotation the gain of the horizontal component of the VOR was the same value as with the head and eyes both straight ahead. In contrast, the time constant of the VOR varied only as a function of the orientation of the head and not as a function of eye position in the orbit. During rotation about an earth vertical axis, the time constant was longest (about 18 s) when the head was pitched forward to place the lateral canals near earth-horizontal and shortest (about 11 s) when the head was pitched backward to place the vertical canals near earth-horizontal.(ABSTRACT TRUNCATED AT 250 WORDS)"

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about the research you're looking for:

## Key Findings on Gaze Aversion During Memory Retrieval

Gaze aversion disengages attention from the world by bringing the gaze away from distractors in order to optimize internal cognitive processes, according to research by Doherty-Sneddon & Phelps (2005) and Glenberg et al. (1998).

When trying to remember an item from memory, people often look away from their immediate environment in order to suppress its distracting effect. In the absence of any social interaction, the frequency of gaze aversion increased as memory task-difficulty increased, as demonstrated in Glenberg et al.'s research.

Gaze aversion during memory retrieval has been described as searching for the answer on the ceiling or in the sky (Doherty-Sneddon & Phelps, 2005).

We spontaneously look away from the face of an interlocutor during cognitively demanding activity; while gaze aversion occurs very little when listening to another person speak, it predominantly occurs while thinking, potentially reflecting the need to concentrate on drawing information from memory and engage in online cognitive processing.

The research demonstrates that averting the gaze facilitates memory retrieval by reducing interference from visual distractors in the external environment, allowing cognitive resources to focus on internal memory processes.

Perfect! I found the study you're searching for. Based on the search results, here's what I found:

The study analyzed eye, head and gaze displacements to 10 degrees, 20 degrees, 30 degrees, 40 degrees, and 50 degrees target presentations in 10 normal subjects. Head displacement was recorded without mechanical restraint by a newly devised apparatus containing two terrestrial magnetic sensors. Eye movements were recorded using d.c. electro-oculography through bitemproal leads.

Key findings from the research include:

- As the target angle increased, the latency of eye movement increased while that of head movements decreased.
- The rise times of eye and head movements increased almost linearly with the target angle.

The paper was published in Acta Otolaryngologica in September-October 1980 (90(3-4):191-8). The authors are T Uemura, Y Arai, and C Shimazaki.

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about cinematography panning guidelines:

## The Seven Second Pan Rule

The rule of thumb is to pan no faster than a full image width every seven seconds, otherwise judder will become too detrimental. This rule is especially simple and powerful because it applies regardless of camera lens, model or sensor size.

## Historical Origin and Context

When shooting at 24/25 FPS with a standard lens, if an object needs to travel from the left edge of the frame to the right edge, the pan should take about seven seconds to get an acceptable looking pan. The longer the lens gets though the slower the pan needs to be.

## Technical Basis

The seven second rule of thumb is based on traditional theatrical viewing at 24 fps with a 180° shutter angle. Varying either setting can influence the appearance of panning and change the optimal panning speed.

## Judder and Motion Artifacts

Critical panning speeds cause judder, which is caused by the stroboscopic effect of moving images and frame rate. Options to address this include increasing motion blur or changing the speed of the movement.

## Important Caveats

Images will not immediately become unwatchable faster than seven seconds, nor will they become fully artifact-free when panning slower than this limit—it's just a good starting point.

Based on the search results, I have found comprehensive information to answer your query. Here's what I found:

## Head-Mounted Camera POV Footage and Viewer Nausea

Your eyes stabilize naturally through the vestibulo-ocular reflex, but the camera records every snap turn. This fundamental mismatch is at the core of why head-mounted camera footage often feels nauseating to viewers.

### The Vestibulo-Ocular Reflex (VOR) Problem

The vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) is a reflex that acts to stabilize gaze during head movement, producing eye movements in the direction opposite that of head movement—for example, when the head moves to the right, the eyes move to the left, meaning the image a person sees stays the same even though the head has turned.

However, with the head-mounted display, the viewer's gaze direction should not be compensated for as the viewer continues to gaze at the HMD image because the HMD and the viewed image move with the head motion, causing vestibular and visual information to conflict with each other, which may be related to the motion sickness experienced by HMD wearers.

### Sensory Conflict and Motion Sickness

These symptoms are commonly referred to as cybersickness, and are thought to be from conflicting visual, vestibular, and proprioceptive signals. More broadly, when visual and vestibular cues disagree—such as during virtual reality, turbulent motion, or certain neurological conditions—people can experience motion sickness, disorientation, or perceptual disturbances.

### Why Stabilization Helps: Steadicam vs. Head-Mounted Footage

When using a high end stabilization system, like a Steadicam, we are able to eliminate any trace of camera shake and replace it with a floating sensation, as if the viewer is flying through the scene. This works because the stabilization removes the jarring, rapid head movements that a viewer's vestibular system is detecting conflict with, creating a smoother visual presentation that doesn't trigger the same sensory mismatch.

### Additional Contributing Factors

Most action cams default to ultra-wide FOV (140°+), which distorts peripheral vision and exaggerates motion—making footage feel disorienting or nauseating in playback.

# Eye-Head Coordination Study Data

The abstract reports these key findings for lateral gaze at different target angles (10-50 degrees):

**Latency:** "As the target angle increased, the latency of eye movement increased while that of head movements decreased."

**Rise Times:** "The rise times of eye and head movements increased almost linearly with the target angle."

**Eye Velocity:** "The maximum velocity of eye movement averaged 292 degrees/sec at 10 degrees, increased to 398 degrees/sec at 20 degrees, and remained unchanged thereafter."

**Head Velocity:** "The maximum velocity of head movement increased linearly with the target angle."

**Head Displacement at Saccade End:** "The head displacement at the end of eye saccade became relatively larger as the target angle increased."

**Final Position - Head Contribution:** "At the final resting position, the percentage of target angle attained by head displacement decreased from 93% at 10 degrees to 62% at 50 degrees."

The study confirms that combined eye-head gaze displacement accurately matched target angles across all tested positions.

I found several relevant sources about head rotation in VR and 360-degree video viewing. Here are the key findings:

**Head Rotation Angular Velocity Characteristics:**

The probability density function of head rotation pitch and roll angles can be modeled as Gaussian distributions, while the probability density function of yaw angles can be modeled as a Gaussian mixture distribution.

**Head Movement Speed Distribution:**

Viewers have different head movement and angular speed patterns, with some viewers tending to be still and rotating their head occasionally compared to others. Head movement speed was measured at time intervals of 1 second, calculated as the great-circle distance between two head orientation samples divided by the total time.

**Natural Head Movements in VR:**

When viewing landscapes, observers rotated their head in line with the image rotation, presumably to make saccades in cardinal, rather than oblique, directions. A VR device outputs the direction of the current viewpoint of the user on the 360° view sphere up to 250 times per second.

**Available Datasets:**

The 360-degree video head movement dataset was documented by Corbillon, X., De Simone, F., Simon, G. This appears to be a foundational dataset for this research area.

The search results indicate there is academic research on head rotation velocity distributions in VR contexts, though the results don't contain specific percentile values or peak yaw velocity measurements in degrees per second that might be in your dataset.

Below are the numbers, all at the project's 30 fps (verified in src/Root.tsx:24 and src/pov/headTurn.ts:47), 1920x1080 logic, 53 deg hFOV = 36.2 px/deg.\n\nEASE FUNCTION (two-bell model, C1 continuous, implementable in headTurn.ts and in the Python renderer identically). One raised-cosine velocity bell for the main movement plus a second, wider, lower bell for the VOR/comfort tail:\n  bell(t; A, T) = (A/T) * ( t - (T/(2*pi)) * sin(2*pi*t/T) )   for 0<=t<=T, = A for t>T, = 0 for t<0\n  velocity of a bell: v(t) = (2A/T) * (1 - cos(2*pi*t/T))/2, peak = 2A/T at t = T/2\n  yaw(t) = bell(t; A1, T1) + bell(t - t2; A2, T2)\n\nTURN-AWAY (44 deg right, 'moderate, natural'):\n  A1 = 38 deg, T1 = 0.38 s  -> peak 200 deg/s at t = 0.19 s\n  A2 = 6 deg,  t2 = 0.25 s, T2 = 0.45 s -> tail peak 27 deg/s\n  total 0.70 s = 21 frames; velocity peak at 27% of total; overshoot 0. If he insists on overshoot: max 0.5 deg (1%), single-sided, folded into the tail (A2 = 6.5 then -0.5 over the last 0.2 s); never a decaying oscillation.\n  For a brisker read use T1 = 0.32 s (237 deg/s); for lazier use T1 = 0.45 s (169 deg/s). Do not exceed ~260 deg/s on a screen: stabilised POV film (Hardcore Henry) and the missing viewer VOR both argue for the low end of the physiological range.\n  Pitch: same bell shape scaled; target 0 to +2 deg (recall gaze aversion is level-to-up) rather than -4 deg, unless the window sightline needs the drop. Compose Fick order: yaw about the WORLD vertical first, then pitch about the turned horizontal axis, roll last.\n  Roll: 0 deg structurally; add a per-turn held value drawn once from N(0, 1.5 deg) (inside the measured 2 deg torsional scatter), ramped in with the same bell. Optional 0.3-0.5 deg transient lean during the acceleration phase is my belief, not sourced.\n  Eye translation (phase 2, depth-guided only): pivot 7.5 cm behind the cyclopean eye (range 7-8.5). x_lat = 7.5*sin(yaw) = 5.2 cm at 44 deg; z_back = 7.5*(1-cos(yaw)) = 2.1 cm. Monitor-vs-wall parallax 3.4 deg = ~120 px at 1080p. Without depth this cannot be faked with a 2D shift; leave translation at zero in phase 1 rather than approximating it.\n\nBLINK (both turns, physiologically 97% for >33 deg; if he wants none on the return, log it as a style exception):\n  onset = head-onset frame minus 1 (-33 ms; EMG leads by 39 ms, lid visibly moving at head onset)\n  close 2 frames (67 ms) -> closed 1 frame (33 ms) -> open 5 frames (167 ms): 8 frames = 267 ms, asymmetric, fast down / slow up. Lid = soft-edged top-down wipe with darkening, not an opacity fade. The design's 3-frame version is inside a partial gaze-evoked blink's closure but has no reopening; extend it.\n\nEYES-LEAD CUE (the eye itself is never rendered): move the peripheral-softness / focus centre toward the turn side at t = 0 (the saccade lands ~100 ms in), then recentre it as the head arrives (0.4-0.7 s). Time the 'Look Away' reveal to the eye's arrival (~0.15-0.25 s), not to the head's 60% mark, if it is to feel found rather than delivered.\n\nTURN-BACK (goal-driven return to a known target: head lag ~30 ms, more direct):\n  A1 = 40 deg, T1 = 0.36 s (222 deg/s); A2 = 4 deg, t2 = 0.22 s, T2 = 0.35 s; total 0.57 s = 17 frames; ends at exactly yaw 0. Same blink rule.\n\nHALF-TURN (card beats, 20 deg):\n  A1 = 17 deg, T1 = 0.30 s (113 deg/s); A2 = 3 deg, t2 = 0.20 s, T2 = 0.35 s; total 0.55 s. Note a 20 deg HEAD move is a lot for a 20 deg gaze shift (real heads do 5-15 deg); it reads as a deliberate glance, which is what the card beat wants.\n\nTIMING RELATIVE TO A CUE: if a cue triggers the turn, start the head 200-375 ms after the cue (human reaction times to in-view vs cued targets).\n\nMOTION BLUR (the item most likely to break realism as designed): samples per frame n = ceil(streak_px / 4), streak_px = 0.5 * delta_yaw_frame * 36.2 (x2 at --scale 2). At the 200 deg/s peak that is 30 samples at 1080p and 60 at 4K, not 4. Cheapest equivalent for a rotation-only camera: per-column horizontal box blur of length L(phi) = 0.5 * delta_yaw * f * sec^2(phi) applied after reprojection, or render the turn at 240 fps and box-average 4 frames per output frame (= 180 deg shutter). Keep the peripheral softness and the motion blur as separate passes so the still frames stay sharp.\n\nHOLDS: keep the existing living drift (a resting head is never perfectly still), but during the 0.7 s turn suppress noise on roll/pitch; the stabilised-helmet lesson is that jitter during a deliberate turn reads as a camera, not a head.\n\nSources: Sidenmark & Gellersen 2019 (https://eprints.lancs.ac.uk/id/eprint/136779/1/Eye_Head_and_Torso_Coordination_During_Gaze_Shifts_in_Virtual_Reality.pdf); Hu et al. CHI 2026 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06164); Freedman 2008 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2605952/); Uemura 1980 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7468181/); Stahl 1999 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10333006/); Fuller 1992 (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00230391); Guitton & Volle 1987 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3655876/); whole-body gaze shifts (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4369292/); truncated Fick gimbal 2024 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11687854/); head rotation axes 2007 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17898142/); Evinger 1994 (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00227203); Gandhi & Bonadonna 2012 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3262917/); blinks and head movements 2025 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13345164/); blink review (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10466800/); Zangemeister main sequence (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7449898/, paywalled); gaze aversion review (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0001691823002172); RED pan-speed rule (https://www.reddigitalcinema.com/red-101/camera-panning-speed); Hardcore Henry rig (https://www.fxguide.com/fxfeatured/how-hardcore-henrys-pov-shots-were-made/); Playtank first-person camera (https://playtank.io/2023/05/12/first-person-3cs-camera/); Mirror's Edge (https://www.gameanim.com/2010/11/05/creating-first-person-movement-for-mirrors-edge/).\n\nNot done: no Kling, Firecrawl or other credits were spent (the firecrawl CLI was checked but not run against the web); no project file was modified. The Zangemeister 1981 human head main-sequence table was not retrievable (ScienceDirect 403, no abstract anywhere), so the 44 deg peak-velocity bracket is interpolated from Uemura's linear scaling and the 90 deg / volitional-turn values, not quoted.", "risks": ["The 40-45 deg human head peak velocity is interpolated (180-250 deg/s), not quoted: the primary source (Zangemeister, Jones & Stark 1981) is paywalled with no abstract.
